The split points a tree may use are computed from the predictors, the cut rule and the cut count when a sampler is made, and are then held while the predictors change, so they are neither scratch, which could be recomputed, nor state: they are data, first derived from what the caller supplied. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "the predictors may change without changing the cut points. That actually makes them a form of 'data', albeit one that is initially derived from the user input. The same could probably be said of the other elements you mention", those being a linear or Gaussian-process leaf's covariate centring and scaling and the default lengthscale. They therefore belong on the data object, and a saved state does not install them. Replacing the data must not change them ambiguously: "You don't want to update derived data in an ambiguous case. It needs to be explicit, because a caller might not understand that setData is changing a different part of the sampler." The rule for setData, in the maintainer's words: "It seems like a 'default' behavior would be to re-derive if no values are present (to recreate old behavior) and if values are supplied, use them. If explicit arguments are given, we could then follow them directly." So a plain setData on a data object that carries no derived values derives them from the new observations, as 0.9-34 did; one that carries them uses them; and an argument naming what to do overrides both. The alternatives put to the maintainer were holding the derived values on every plain setData, with arguments to update them, and requiring the arguments on every call. Not ruled here: the arguments' names and values, whether the response range moves from the model to the data object to follow the same rule, which dec-B232 then ruled, and what governs the leaf standardization, which dec-B233 then ruled. See also: [dec-B200], [dec-B230], [dec-B232], [dec-B233].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B231]

**The response range is recorded on the data object**
The response range, computed from the response when a sampler is made and held when the response is swapped without re-anchoring, is the same kind of value as the split points of dec-B231, so its record moves from the model, where dec-B200 put it, to the data object, and setData treats it as it treats the split points: derived when the new data object carries none, used when it carries one, and an explicit argument overriding both. What dec-B200 ruled about behaviour stands: a state stored under another range is converted into the sampler's as it is installed, and no state install moves the range. The alternative was leaving the record on the model, which gives setData one rule for the range and another for the split points, and lets a data object taken from one sampler bring its split points to another but not its range. The costs named to the maintainer were small: the home part of a ruling a few days old is revised, and a range, like split points, is derived under the settings of the sampler that made it (its family and offset), so a data object carried to a sampler with other settings brings a value a fresh derivation would not give. The maintainer on 2026-10-05, asked whether it moves: "I think so, right? Why not?"
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B232]

**The leaf covariate standardization has its own argument**
A linear or Gaussian-process leaf's covariate centring and scaling, and the default lengthscale derived with them, are refreshed only when the caller names them, through an argument of their own on setData and setPredictor, beside updateCutPoints for the split points and updateScale for the response range: three arguments for three parts of the sampler. On setPredictor it defaults to keeping them, as updateCutPoints does; on setData, not given, it follows dec-B231's rule. The argument is updateStandardization: the orchestrator's first working name, updateLeafScale, would have read as updating the leaf prior's scale, which the model object already calls leaf.scale, and of updateStandardization, updateCovariateScale and updateLeafCovariates the maintainer on 2026-10-05 took the first: "updateStandardization is fine." The records on the data object are named cutPoints, standardization and response.range, the orchestrator's proposal made with it. The alternatives were folding it into updateCutPoints, so that a caller refreshing split points would also move the slope prior and kernel, and having no argument, the standardization fixed at creation and replaced only by setData, as glmnet, rstanarm, brms, flexBART and kernlab fix theirs for the life of a fit. Leaf models are new in 1.0-0, so no released behaviour changes. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "For the updateLeafScale question, have it be its own argument."
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B233]

**setState returns whether the restore was exact, and does not warn**
setState invisibly returns TRUE when every tree was installed as stored and FALSE when any was changed on the way in: leaves no row reaches merged, splits moved to the nearest current split point, or a missing-value direction dropped, which happen when the sampler's predictors or split points changed after the state was stored. Code that stores the state, proposes a change and restores on rejection can test the value, as one tests optim's convergence code; an inexact rejection there is quietly wrong, and one user had asked on the issue tracker whether forced merges were accounted for. No warning is raised, so the restore stays as quiet as it was in 0.9-34; copy() and a reload, which have no return value to carry it, stay silent. No caller read the previous return value, NULL. The alternatives were the status plus a classed warning on every inexact restore, the convention of glm, survival, mgcv, lme4 and rstan, and silence with the manual's ordering rule as the only guard. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Return a status, no warning."
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05; revised by dec-B305. [dec-B234]

**The quantile split points are spread over the whole column, on every entry point**
With useQuantiles = TRUE a column's split points are a thinned set of the midpoints between its distinct values, and the thinning rule inherited from BayesTree through 0.9-34 stepped through them by an integer step, so the points ran out before the top of the column whenever the number of distinct values was not a clean multiple of the cut count: at 100 cuts, a third of the distinct values lay above the highest split point at 150 distinct values, half at 199, a tenth at 999, and no tree could split there; a refresh through setPredictor(updateCutPoints = TRUE) did the same at any size. The split points are now taken at evenly spaced positions among the midpoints, still midpoints as documented, at creation and at refresh, on every entry point, bartBT included; a column with no more distinct values than the cut count plus one uses every midpoint as before, so such fits do not change. On Friedman's function at 150 and 199 rows the old rule's test error was 1.26 and 1.90 times the new rule's, and on the Boston housing data 3.60 against 3.12; from 300 rows the two agreed. Fits that ask for quantiles on a column with more distinct values than that change, one recorded baseline is re-made, and the comparison against 0.9-34 gains an explained difference. The alternatives were fixing the modern entry points only and leaving bartBT on BayesTree's rule, which leaves the flaw with the 0.9-x callers who write usequants = TRUE and makes that spelling behave two ways, since pdbart sends it to the modern function; adopting the BART package's data-weighted quantiles, whose points can coincide with observed values and repeat; and restoring only 0.9-34's refresh warning. Only BayesTree and pgbart share the old arithmetic; BART, XGBoost, LightGBM and scikit-learn cover the whole column. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Fix it everywhere, bartBT included." See also: [dec-B83].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B235]

**fitted takes what extract takes**
fitted is the average over draws of what extract returns, so a type extract supports for a per-observation quantity is a type fitted supports: fitted(type = "forest") on a fit with several forests returns the posterior mean of each forest's own total, one column per forest, where the agents had left it out (dec-A114). The default stays a single vector, usually the expected value, or the linear part of the model, depending on the context. A multinomial fit keeps refusing the type on fitted, as on extract and predict, a category's forest not being identified. The alternative was keeping per-forest values to extract and predict, as mgcv keeps per-term values to predict, on the ground that a forest's total before its basis and amplitude multiply it is not its contribution to the fitted value; the manual says that of the type instead. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Generally, fitted is just the average of extract, so whatever it supports we like to support too. I see now reason not to allow 'forest' as a type. That said, it's default interpretation should always be of a single vector that is usually the expected value, or the linear part of the model, depending on the context. Some natural caveats apply." The orchestrator read "I see now reason" as "I see no reason", from the sentence before it, and read the caveats as covering what is not a value per observation (trees, sigma, k, the leaf prior's sd, variable counts, a shape, thresholds), the pointwise log-likelihood, and posterior predictive draws that are category codes, none of which fitted gains. The maintainer, told both readings: "I think your interpretation of fitted's desired functionality is correct."
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B236]

**A gp-leaf sampler that holds kept draws refuses a change of standardization, for the release**
A kept draw from a Gaussian-process leaf belongs to the kernel it was drawn under, whose width in real units is the covariate scale times the lengthscale, so it cannot be converted when the leaf covariate standardization or default lengthscale changes, as a linear leaf's kept slopes can. For 1.0-0 a gp-leaf sampler holding kept draws refuses such a change, naming the remedy: setData on a data object that carries no standardization, and setData or setPredictor with updateStandardization = TRUE, stop and say to pass updateStandardization = FALSE or make a new sampler; a state whose kept gp draws were made under another standardization is refused as one under another named lengthscale already is. Until now those draws were replayed through the new standardization without a word, off by 8.5 in a probe on a response with standard deviation 2. Afterwards each kept gp draw is to store the centre, scale and lengthscale it was drawn under and replay under them, so nothing is refused; that is about 350 lines against 30 and adds a block to the saved state, and it is to be conditional, carried only where it is needed. The record itself is small, three numbers per leaf covariate per kept draw beside the draw's own stored rows; the cost is in code. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Option 1 for release, option 2 later. I assume keeping the kernel is expensive, so it should be made appropriately conditional." See also: [dec-A146], [dec-B200], [dec-B231], [dec-B233].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B237]

**A leaf is empty only when no row reaches it, switched-off rows counted**
The sampler rejects a tree move that would leave a leaf empty (dec-A12). What counts as empty, when rows carry zero weight or are masked out by setActiveRows, goes back to 0.9-34's meaning: a leaf is empty only if no row at all reaches it. A leaf holding only switched-off rows is legal, contributes nothing to the likelihood, and has its value drawn from the prior. The branch had counted only switched-in rows, which made a fixed zero weight the same as deleting the row but made the set of allowed trees depend on the mask. The mask exists for a larger sampler that redraws which rows are in every sweep, and that sampler's own step is right only if the tree prior does not move with the mask. Measured against the exact posterior of such a model, ten rows, a two-part mixture with membership redrawn every sweep: under the branch's rule the long-run membership probabilities were off by up to 0.066 and the fitted values by a quarter of a posterior standard deviation, with trees too small; counting switched-off rows, every quantity was within Monte Carlo error. At 300 rows and 50 trees the two rules differed by up to 0.044 in a membership probability, in rows where a region was mostly switched off, and by under 0.005 in 290 of 300 rows. With no zero weight and no mask the two give the same draws in a single-forest fit. The costs: a fixed zero weight is no longer identical to deleting the row, since trees may isolate a region of switched-off rows, whose fit then comes from the prior, so the manual says a zero-weight or masked row is in the design but not in the likelihood; and results change for fits with zero weights or a mask. The alternatives were keeping the branch's rule and documenting that a redrawn mask is approximate, and counting switched-off rows under a mask but not under zero weights, two rules over what is one mechanism on a gaussian fit. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Count switched-off rows." See also: [dec-A12], [dec-A116].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B238]

**Model configuration moves off the control**
A fitted sampler's control carried, on hidden attributes, facts that are model or data and not run settings: a variance forest's tree count, tree prior and columns, the forests of a multi-forest fit and their settings, the number of ordinal categories, the negative-binomial shape specification, the hazard periods, and the survival status, one value per row. Each was attached as its feature was built, the control being the object already handed to the engine, while the model object carries its own attached facts (the family, monotone directions, interaction constraints, the residual degrees of freedom). The five model facts move to the model object and the survival status to the data object, beside the weights and the offset, so a control holds run settings only and passing one fit's control to another call needs no special handling, as base R's glm, nls, mgcv and lme4 controls need none. No draw changes, and none of stan4bart, bartCause and treatSens reads the attributes. The question arose from dec-A120, where bart and xbart refused a control carrying such facts while dbarts dropped them silently; the alternative was leaving them on the control and dropping them silently everywhere. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "I'd rather do a clean migration at the object level and leave tombstones or something (kinda hard on data members). SO yeah, move them to the model and data objects." The control's documented slots that are arguably model (the binary-response flag, the number of trees, useQuantiles) are ruled in dec-B240. See also: [dec-A120], [dec-B195], [dec-B231], [dec-B240].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B239]

**The control's three 0.9-x model slots move too**
The control has three documented slots, inherited from 0.9-x, that are model or data and not run settings: binary, the flag that the response is binary; n.trees, the number of trees; and useQuantiles, the rule that places split points at quantiles. Under dec-B239 the hidden model facts leave the control, and these three go with them: the number of trees to the model object, the quantile rule to the data object beside the cut counts and the cut points it derives (dec-B231), and the binary flag is what the model's family already says. R has no hook on a slot read, so control@n.trees cannot warn; the old slots stay as mirrored copies until 1.1-0, the only tombstone a data member can have, and dbartsControl's n.trees and useQuantiles arguments fall under dec-A02's rule for a relocated argument. The cost: every caller needs a new spelling, 0.9-x scripts that name them on dbartsControl warn for one release, and the three consumer packages change where they read or write them (bartCause reads control@binary and control@n.trees, treatSens writes control@binary). The alternative, which the orchestrator recommended, was leaving the three on the control as documented 0.9-x arguments. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "move them too." Where a dbarts caller names them afterwards, and whether the control's cut count moves as well, are ruled in dec-B241. See also: [dec-A02], [dec-B231], [dec-B239], [dec-B241].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B240]

**The tree count is a forest's; the cut count and quantile rule are the data's**
With the number of trees, the cut count and the quantile rule off the control (dec-B240), a dbarts caller needs a spelling for each. The number of trees is a property of a forest: forest(n.trees = ) names it, as it already does for every forest of a multi-forest fit and as varianceForest(n.trees = ) does for the variance forest, the model object holds it per forest, so a single-forest model is one forest declaration, and dbarts gains a plain n.trees as shorthand for the first forest's, refused when both are given, as the top-level interactions and blocks already are and as bart's n.trees reads. The cut count and the quantile rule describe the predictors, since the sampler keeps one set of split points per column for all forests and split points are data (dec-B231): they are plain n.cuts and useQuantiles arguments on dbarts and dbartsData, as on bart. The control's cut-count slot goes as well, with no mirrored copy: the branch added it, and 0.9-34 routed dbartsControl's n.cuts to the data object. The orchestrator had first offered the tree count on the tree prior, as cgm(n.trees = ); the maintainer on 2026-10-05: "The tree prior isn't right - they are properties for the forest." The other alternatives were the forest object alone, which makes the most common 0.9-x customisation a nested call, and a grid per forest, which the sampler does not have. Shown the forest-object-plus-shorthand spelling and the data arguments as the recommendation, the maintainer on 2026-10-05: "OK, use your current recommendations for these items." Under it the orchestrator's calls, not named in the ruling: a single forest may be given as a bare forest() without list(), and dbartsSpec takes the same n.trees shorthand. See also: [dec-B231], [dec-B239], [dec-B240].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B241]

**treatSens resolves a forwarded treatment model as dbarts resolves a forwarded prior**
treatSens.BART and cibart take the treatment model as a call, trt.model = probit(family = "normal", scale = s), whose constructors treatSens does not export. Under dec-A126 the lockstep branch evaluates that call in the caller's frame with the constructors visible by name, which fixed a local variable inside the call, but a call forwarded through a wrapper still failed with could not find function "probit", through the wrapper's dots or through its own named argument; the released treatSens fails both as well, with treatment model of unrecognized type. treatSens now does what dbarts 1.0 does for a prior constructor: a call forwarded through a wrapper's dots is recovered as written and resolved where it was written, and one forwarded through a wrapper's named argument fails with a message that names the spelling that works there, the model's name as a string. Run on 2026-10-05: dbarts resolves cgm(...) through dots and names dbartsPriors$cgm(...) for the named argument; lm's weights through dots work; glm's family works through any wrapper because binomial is exported. The alternatives were leaving the branch as it was, with a message that does not say what to do, and exporting the constructors, under which treatSens's bart would mask dbarts::bart for anyone attaching both. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Match dbarts." See also: [dec-A75], [dec-A126].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B242]

**stan4bart credits Carpenter and Ward as contributors and the Walnutpie Developers as the only copyright holder**
stan4bart 0.0-14 includes headers copied from the Walnutpie library, which implements the WALNUTS sampler. Under dec-A126 the agents had listed Bob Carpenter as contributor and copyright holder and the Walnutpie Developers as copyright holder. The licence that travels with the code, MIT, reads Copyright (c) 2025--2026 by the Walnutpie Developers and names no person; upstream's commits on 2026-10-05 were Bob Carpenter 259, Brian Ward 177 and two others 11 and 9; CRAN's policy asks for ctb for the authors of included code and cph where someone other than the package authors holds the copyright; Matrix names an included library's author as ctb and its copyright holder separately, and RcppEigen credits Authors of Eigen as cph with no individual. DESCRIPTION now lists Carpenter and Ward as ctb for the Walnutpie headers and the Walnutpie Developers as their only cph. The alternatives were leaving it, which named Carpenter as a copyright holder where the licence does not and omitted Ward, and the Developers alone, which drops Carpenter from a package that credited him for StanHeaders in its released version. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Carpenter and Ward as ctb, the Developers as the only cph." See also: [dec-A126].
Record: stan4bart commit 229901b. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B243]

**bartCause's p.weight estimator divides by the sum of the scores, with or without weights**
bartCause's p.weight method averages the individual effects with propensity-score weights. For the effect on the treated, released 1.0-10 divides the weighted sum by the treated count when no weights are given, the formula the bartc help states and the tmle package's starting value from its first release with that estimand to 2.1.1, and by the weighted sum of the scores when weights are given, a formula in bartCause's first commit, five years before tmle took weights, where tmle divides by the weighted treated count. A fit with no weights and one with all weights equal therefore gave different estimates, and without weights the values extract returns as p.weights did not sum to one; on the lockstep branch the agents had rescaled those values by the sample size, recorded under dec-A126 only as a test tolerance. Both forms estimate the same quantity. Measured on 2026-10-05 over 40 simulated datasets per cell with a BART propensity score, which bartCause fits without the weights: with equal weights the two forms differed by 0.004 at 300 rows and 0.002 at 1000; with unequal weights the root mean squared error against the true effect was 0.207 for both at 300 rows and 0.108 for the treated count against 0.094 for the sum of scores at 1000; the posterior spread under the treated count was 8 to 10 percent wider in every cell; with a glm propensity score, fitted with the weights, the two are identical. Both the estimate and the weights now divide by the sum of the scores in every case, so the weights sum to one in each draw, no weights agrees with equal weights, weighted results do not move, and unweighted att and atc estimates under a BART propensity score move against 1.0-10 by a few percent of a posterior standard deviation. The alternative was the treated count in both, which matches tmle's formula and leaves unweighted results alone but moves weighted ones by about a third of a posterior standard deviation and not toward the truth; the orchestrator had first recommended it. bartCause's built-in targeted estimator, used when tmle is absent or weights are given, keeps its own formula, whose influence curve is derived for it. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Let's use sum of scores in both." See also: [dec-A126].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B244]

**bartCause's weighted targeted estimate comes from the tmle package**
bartCause's tmle method calls the tmle package for each posterior draw when no weights are given, and its own built-in targeted estimator when weights are given or tmle is absent, a guard written when tmle took no weights; tmle has taken them since 2.0.0, in 2023. The built-in code is a transcription of tmle's 2018 estimator for the effect on the treated, faithful without weights; its weighted formulas are bartCause's own and differ from tmle's in the denominator and in the influence curve's second term, it still starts from the fit targeted for the average effect, where tmle since 2.0.0 starts from the initial fit, and it does not trim controls as tmle has since 2.0.1. Run on 2026-10-05 with tmle 2.1.1 on the inputs bartCause supplies: tmle with all weights equal reproduces its unweighted result exactly; the built-in weighted standard error was about 12 times smaller than tmle's, the estimate being converted back to the response scale and the standard error not; and a fit with weights and posteriorOfTMLE = FALSE stops with an error; both defects are in released 1.0-10. A fit with weights now calls tmle with them, and stops with a message asking for tmle 2.0.0 or later when tmle is older or absent; without weights nothing changes, the built-in code still serving when tmle is absent. The costs: weighted tmle estimates and standard errors move against 1.0-10, and a weighted tmle fit needs the suggested package. The alternative was keeping and repairing the built-in weighted code. The maintainer on 2026-10-05, shown the tmle package's weights: "Since tmle added weights, why wouldn't we call them now?"; and shown this plan: "Yes, do it." See also: [dec-B244].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B245]

**A forest takes the same prior arguments as the front door**
A single-forest fit states its forest's spread as leaf.prior = normal(sd = ) or normal(k = ) and its tree prior as tree.prior = cgm(power, base); a multi-forest fit states them per forest as the flat scalars forest(sd = ), forest(base = ) and forest(power = ). Run on 2026-10-05: forest(sd = ) is refused on a single forest and leaf.prior = normal(sd = ) on a multi-forest fit; normal(sd = 2) means 2 in the response's units and forest(sd = 2) two standard deviations of the response, though both name the prior standard deviation of the forest's total contribution; setLeafPrior takes leaf.prior on one kind of sampler and forests on the other; and getLeafPrior reports a prior object on one and a forest() object on the other (dec-A127). The flat scalars are the ones the front door shed onto the prior constructors, and the reference bcf package states its two spreads in the response's units, with defaults written as multiples of the response's standard deviation. forest() now takes tree.prior and leaf.prior in the front door's vocabulary, in place of base, power and sd, a forest's sd in the response's units as everywhere else; the top-level tree.prior and leaf.prior are the first forest's, refused when both are given, as dec-B241 rules for the tree count; getLeafPrior returns a prior object for every forest, keeping the entry that says what the sd is the sd of; setLeafPrior takes one form, a leaf prior and the forest it is for, and its forests argument goes; and what a multi-forest sampler cannot honour, a k-named prior among them, stays refused by name. forest() is new in 1.0-0, so nothing released changes. The alternatives were leaving the two vocabularies as built, and moving forest(sd = ) to the response's units alone, which keeps two spellings, two writer arguments and two reader shapes. The cost is a rework of the multi-forest surface and of bartCause's causal-forest calls before the release, designed first so that the per-forest record of dec-B239 is built once. The maintainer on 2026-10-05, asked what the reader should report: "Should we be taking this opportunity to make something more consistent, or have a cleaner interface?"; and shown the three options: "A forest takes the same prior arguments as the front door." Supersedes dec-B142's forests argument and the reader's forest(sd = ) report of dec-A124 and dec-A127. See also: [dec-B141], [dec-B142], [dec-A124], [dec-A127], [dec-B239], [dec-B241].
Record: this register. Marked: revised by the maintainer, 2026-10-06: forest(sd = ) is the documented spelling and leaf.prior = normal(sd = ) the long form, under dec-B266; one unit and one reader and writer shape stand. [dec-B246]

**The tree count of a live sampler does not change; a warm start is the path between counts**
In 0.9-34 the sampler's setControl, given a control with another n.trees, resized the sampler in place: run on CRAN's 0.9-34 on 2026-10-05, a 50-tree sampler set to 20 and to 120 trees ran on with that count, the first trees kept, the rest dropped or added, and the leaf prior rescaled. On the branch it is refused (changing 'n.trees' is not available on an existing sampler), which the help states but NEWS and this register did not, and which was not put to the maintainer when it was built. A different chain count was refused by 0.9-34 too. None of bartCause, stan4bart, treatSens and bairrtt calls setControl, and none of the nine CRAN reverse dependencies in a local snapshot does. The refusal stays and NEWS records it as a change from 0.9-34: in BART the tree count is part of the prior, so a resized chain is another model needing its own burn-in, and restoring the resize meant sampler work on live chains, the variance forest, multi-forest fits and stored draws for a feature with no known user. The alternative was restoring it. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Keep the refusal and record it in NEWS as a change from 0.9-34. But maybe we can add only partially populating trees from another model in the warm-starts to handle the big -> small, small -> big scenarios." A warm start from a donor with another tree count is refused today as not shape-compatible; relaxing that is queued in TODO as warm-start-tree-count. See also: [dec-B240], [dec-B241].
Record: this register; inst/NEWS.Rd; TODO. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B247]

**setModel changes what is safe and sensible to change on a live sampler**
The sampler's setModel installs a new model object on a live sampler. Some model facts were treated as fixed at creation, each by its own rule: a monotone constraint that differs or is missing was refused by name, an interaction limit or a Student-t setting that differs was accepted and then lost or ignored, so a copy or a reload ran another model than the live sampler (run on 2026-10-05). The orchestrator put two uniform rules to the maintainer, refusing every difference, or keeping what a model does not state and refusing what it states differently; the maintainer pointed out that an empty constraint is also a statement ("What do you mean 'does not state'? Isn't empty also an implication?"), which removes the second, a model object being a complete description. The rule is by fact and by use, not uniform: setModel supports a change where it is safe on a running chain and has a legitimate use, and refuses the rest by name. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "In general for the model setting question, I'd say that we should support setting them where it is safe and sensible to do so. Generally, no one wants to swap out an entire response distribution, and it would pretty uncommon to change a prior family. We should think about what makes actual sense and had legitimate uses cases. We might also need to expand 'warm starts' to include things like fitting a linear model on a transformed response and using it to see a slower model on the original scale. Presumably, some sense of scale or dispersion might need to be translated. That said, that's future work (post 1.0)." Which facts fall on which side is not ruled here; a warm start across models is queued in TODO for after 1.0-0. See also: [dec-A128], [dec-B239], [dec-B247].
Record: this register; TODO. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B248]

**The forest column follows the kind of model, not how the call was written**
dec-A96 gives the forest column of getTrees and extract(type = "trees") to a multinomial fit and to "a fit declared with forests, whatever its count". Under dec-B241 forests = forest(n.trees = 50) is the ordinary way to name a single forest's tree count and the plain n.trees its shorthand, so the two spellings of one single-forest model would have returned different tables: run on 2026-10-05, a plain fit returns tree, n, var, value and a fit declared with one forest returns forest, tree, n, var, value. The column now appears on a multinomial fit and on a fit whose forests are combined through amplitudes, and on no other, however the call was written; a single-forest fit returns 0.9-34's columns. The alternative was keeping dec-A96's wording, under which the sampler records which spelling the caller used and naming the tree count in its home brings the forest index the maintainer had objected to. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "As before, if the user would never expect multiple forest outputs we shouldn't pretend like there are other forests out there." See also: [dec-A96], [dec-B241].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B249]

**A mirrored control slot stays until no CRAN package reads it**
Under dec-B240 the control keeps mirrored copies of binary, n.trees and useQuantiles "until 1.1-0", R having no hook to warn on a slot read. Searched on 2026-10-05 over the sources of all 27 current CRAN reverse dependencies: bartMan 0.2.1 and tidypredict read fit$control@binary, the second to refuse classification fits, and get no warning in 1.0-0; countSTAR 1.2.1, mcmcsae and nlfh 0.1.0 pass dbartsControl(n.trees = ), which warns once per session (nlfh's call, spread over several lines, was missed by the first search and found by a second, and a third full scan found no more); no other package reads the three slots or passes the three arguments. family(fit) is the 1.0-0 replacement for the slot read. Each mirrored slot stays until no CRAN package reads it, checked at the 1.1-0 freeze, as dec-A02 holds the bart2 alias until no CRAN package calls it; the relocated arguments expire at 1.1-0 with the other tombstones, their users having been warned. The cost is that the mirror code and up to three slots may outlive the other tombstones. The alternative was removing the slots at 1.1-0 with everything else, which breaks the two slot readers unless they have changed, with only a note from the maintainer to warn them. The five packages join the list of authors to contact. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Each mirrored slot stays until no CRAN package reads it, checked at the 1.1-0 freeze." See also: [dec-A02], [dec-B240].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B250]

**The zero-weight warning says what a zero weight does**
When weights holds zeros among other values, dbarts warns 'weights' of 0 will be ignored but increase computation time, 0.9-34's text; 0.9-34 raised it for any zero weight, and the branch stays silent when every weight is 0 or 1, the zeros there being the statement of which rows are in (run on both, 2026-10-05). Under dec-B238 the text is no longer true: a zero-weight row is left out of the likelihood but stays in the fit, occupying a leaf the trees may split around. The warning is reworded to say so and to name subset as the way to drop rows, and the two tests matching the old text move with it. The alternatives were keeping 0.9-34's text, and dropping the warning, which leaves a caller who passes zeros by accident with no hint. A 0.9-x script that matches on the message text no longer matches. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Reword it to what is true." See also: [dec-B238].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B251]

**The variance forest takes a tree prior object**
varianceForest(vars, n.trees, base, power), which describes the residual-variance forest of a heteroscedastic fit, gives its tree prior as two flat numbers, the pattern dec-B246 removed from forest(); that ruling did not name it. It takes tree.prior = cgm(power, base) in their place, defaulting as before to the mean forest's tree prior, so a tree prior has one spelling at the front door, on forest() and on the variance forest; a tree prior the variance forest cannot use is refused by name. It has no leaf prior argument, its leaves having their own fixed prior. varianceForest() is new in 1.0-0, so nothing released changes. The alternative was leaving base and power. The maintainer on 2026-10-05, shown the orchestrator's recommendation of the tree prior object: "Use your recommendation for this decision." See also: [dec-B246].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B252]

**A forest's sd is the size of its contribution to the response**
Under dec-B246 a forest's spread is written forest(leaf.prior = normal(sd = s)) in the response's units. On a single forest s is the prior sd of the forest's total. On a forest of a multi-forest fit the forest's output is multiplied by a coefficient, so s could describe the forest before the multiplication or what it contributes after. The rule: a forest's sd is how large its contribution to the response is expected to be, in the response's units, exactly so on a single forest or where the coefficient is held fixed, and as the prior median where the coefficient is random, the coefficient's job being to make that size uncertain; nothing else a caller sets moves it. A user has beliefs about the sizes of a model's terms, and the forest before its coefficient is not separately identified. It is the reference bcf package's reading, whose help gives sd_control and sd_moderate as SD(mu(x)) and SD(tau(x)) "or its prior median" when the scale is random, and whose source divides by 0.674 only then. Run on 2026-10-05 on a two-forest fit with the treatment forest's sd at 1: rescaling the basis by 3 already leaves the contribution at 1, the forest scaled down to compensate; changing amplitude.prior.variance from 0.5 to 2 or 0.125 changes nothing in the calibration, so it is a second multiplier on the same size; and with update.amplitude = FALSE, the coefficient fixed, the 0.674 correction is still applied and the contribution is 1.48. Two changes follow: no correction where the coefficient is fixed, and amplitude.prior.variance is no longer a scale a caller sets. The rule also answers what extract(type = "leaf.prior.sd") returns on such a fit, the contribution's sd as stated per forest, and the units of bartCause's sd.control and sd.moderate, the response's, as sizes of the two contributions. The alternative was the literal reading, s as the sd of the forest's own total, under which the treatment effect's prior median is 0.674 s and a bcf user's numbers do not carry over. All of it is new in 1.0-0, and the default fit does not move. The maintainer on 2026-10-05, asked which reading: "Again, what makes sense to users? What's the clean design? Should we step back and look at the big picture?"; and shown the rule: "OK, adopt the rule as stated with its two changes." See also: [dec-B246], [dec-A147].
Record: this register. Marked: revised in part by the maintainer, 2026-10-05 (dec-B257): a numeric multiplier's units are the caller's, so a setting other than the sd does move a forest's contribution. [dec-B253]

**setModel changes a model's parameters, never its structure**
dec-B248 ruled that setModel supports what is safe and sensible to change on a live sampler and asked what makes sense. The line: a larger sampler running the BART sampler inside it changes, between sweeps, numbers it owns, ones that could have their own prior and be drawn; it never turns the model into another kind of model. Structure is what names the model: the response family, the kind of leaf and its columns, the forests with their tree counts and columns, the constraints on which trees are allowed (monotone, interaction limits, blocks), whether there is a variance forest, the number of ordinal categories, the hazard periods, and DART against the ordinary tree prior. A chain's trees and latents mean something only under the structure they were drawn in, so a model that differs in structure is refused by name, before anything is stored, and the route is a new sampler, warm-started where that helps. Parameters are the numbers inside the structure, under any value of which the current state stays valid: the tree prior's power and base on any forest, the variance forest included; split probabilities; the leaf scale as k or sd; the residual prior and a residual sd held fixed; the Student-t degrees of freedom; a negative-binomial shape held fixed; a Gaussian-process lengthscale; DART's own settings. Handing a parameter between drawn by the sampler and held at the caller's value counts as a parameter change. A constraint is structure even where loosening it would leave the chain valid, a rule by direction or by where the chain is being harder to predict; the tree-move probabilities are not model and stay with setControl. Run on 2026-10-05, the facts the sampler honours today are the first forest's power and base, split probabilities, the leaf scale in every form, the residual prior and the move probabilities, with five defects among them, one a regression against 0.9-34 (after k is switched between fixed and drawn the live sampler's run output loses or freezes its k column); another family's model, a Student-t change, interaction limits, blocks and Gaussian-process settings are accepted and not honoured, so a copy or a reload runs another model or fails; the rest is refused. The Student-t degrees of freedom, the negative-binomial shape, the Gaussian-process lengthscale and tree priors on forests other than the first are parameters the sampler cannot yet be given, and are built for 1.0-0. The alternative shown first was keeping what works today and refusing the rest, with those four left to the backlog; the maintainer: "I'm concerned less whith what happens today and more with what makes sense." Shown the line and the four to build, the maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Parameters yes, structure no. Build it for 1.0. It can be post main-merged." The work may therefore land after the merge to main and before the release. See also: [dec-B248], [dec-A128], [dec-B247].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B254]

**A weighted tmle fit in bartCause leaves the BART learner out of tmle's refit of the propensity score**
For the effect on the treated, the tmle package adjusts the propensity scores it is handed: when more than a tenth of the controls have a score below the lowest treated score, it refits treatment on the supplied score over the remaining rows with SuperLearner, by default over a glm, a gam and a probit BART learner that passes its observation weights to dbarts::bart. dbarts 1.0 refuses weights other than 0 and 1 on a probit fit, so with weights that learner fails in every fold and SuperLearner carries on with the other two, printing the error 11 times per posterior draw; under 0.9-34 it printed nothing. Run on 2026-10-05 with tmle 2.1.1: equal weights of 1 or 3 reproduce the unweighted fit exactly, and equal weights of 0.7 do not, tmle's normalization landing one rounding step from 1, the estimate moving by 0.039 at 100 rows. bartCause now names tmle's library for that refit as the glm and the gam when weights are given, which is what ran before without the errors, and calls tmle without weights when all weights are equal, so equal weights of any value give the unweighted fit. Without weights tmle's default library stays. The alternatives were bartCause naming the glm and the gam always, under which weights never change the set of models but unweighted results move wherever tmle refits; a BART learner that ignores the weights, as bartCause's own propensity model does and as nine of sixteen SuperLearner 2.0-40 learners read do, which SuperLearner can find only by name from inside tmle, so only when bartCause is attached and exports it; and leaving the errors with a note in the help. The cost is that a weighted and an unweighted fit use different sets of models there, so weights that are nearly equal do not approach the unweighted answer smoothly. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Use your recommendation for this item." See also: [dec-B245].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B255]

**An interrupted fit raises R's interrupt, and the sampler stays where it stopped**
A fit stopped with Ctrl-C raises R's interrupt condition, as base R's own long computations and every package using Rcpp's check do: nothing is printed as an error, try() and error handlers do not catch it, so a loop or script around the fit stops too, and tryCatch(interrupt = ) sees it. The interrupted call returns nothing, and a sampler object the caller holds is left at a valid point of its chain and can be run again. Run on 2026-10-05, a loop of three fits in try() interrupted during the first: 0.9-34 could not be stopped until the run finished, 36.7 seconds for a run interrupted at 5, and then raised a true interrupt, so the loop stopped; the branch stopped within a tenth of a second but raised an error, "sampler run interrupted", which try() swallowed, and the other two fits ran to their end. The alternatives were keeping the error, which a script can react to but which leaves a user unable to stop a loop of fits wrapped in try(), and returning the draws made so far with a warning, which no R fitting function does and which lets a script carry on with a shortened fit. The cost is a change wherever a run returns to R, the flat C entry point included, whose callers saw an R error and now see an interrupt. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Yeah, there's no expectation that partial draws be returned. We can do option 1." The orchestrator's call under it, told to the maintainer with the ruling and not ruled: where the interrupt check sits inside a pass over the trees (a slow monotone count, large logistic counts, a latent refresh), the sampler stops with that pass partly applied and is not rolled back to its start; continuing gives a correct chain that is not, draw for draw, the run that would have happened, and a rollback would cost every fit a copy of every tree on every pass. See also: [dec-A128], [dec-B149].
Record: docs/plans/interrupt-is-interrupt.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-05. [dec-B256]

**A forest that multiplies a numeric column has an absolute sd, which the column then scales; the package neither centres nor scales the column**
In a model with a term w * f2(x), the sd stated for that forest is the prior sd of the forest itself, in the response's units, as on any other forest, the median size where its coefficient is drawn and exact where it is held (dec-B253); the column then scales it, so the term at a row has prior sd |w| times the stated one. It is not defined relative to the column: the maintainer, asked whether it should be per unit of w, "why are we specifying a parameter relative to the scale of the vector being multiplied. Why is it not an sd in an absolute sense, that then gets scaled by the vector?" The package does not divide the column by anything and does not centre it; a caller who wants a prior per standard deviation, or a forest that means the response at the average w, standardizes the column, and the help says so, shows how, and may give the constants other packages use (the maintainer: "If you want to state in the help what other packages do (their constants and all that), you can."). For a factor the sd stays the difference between two levels (dec-B253), which a two-valued column one unit apart, 0/1 or -0.5/+0.5, then shares. The branch had divided by the median absolute value of the column over its nonzero rows, recomputed whenever the column was swapped; the maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Your proposal feels very ad-hoc. It's common to scale by sd(y) / sd(x) and the like, but bringing in the median of the absolute value of something - I've never seen that be a comon option", and, of dec-B253's "nothing else a caller sets moves it", "I don't care that an option might go against what I said earlier." Read from their sources on 2026-10-05: stochtree 0.4.5, SoftBart 1.0.3, brms 2.23.0, mgcv 1.9-4 and R-INLA use the multiplier as given and state a prior per unit; VCBART 1.2.5, flexBART 2.0.6, rstanarm 2.32.2's stan_glm and dbarts's own linear leaves divide by the sd and also centre; arm 1.15-3 divides by two sds, centres, and keeps a two-valued input as a one-unit contrast; no package read scales without centring. Built and run the same day: a sd rule with a two-valued exception lets one stray value multiply the prior on a treatment effect by 2.0 to 7.1; recomputing a data-derived scale on a swap changed the coefficient's sd from 0.77 to 0.25 between w and 3 w inside one chain; with a column 10 sds from zero every uncentred fit covered a 90 percent interval about 0.2 of the time, whatever the scale. The alternatives were a single derived rule, the root mean square difference between two rows whose multiplier differs, which gives a two-valued column its contrast at any share, a continuous one 1.41 sds and the factor its law with no special case, and of which the maintainer said "It might be a better option, but we don't have the space to justify it here", no package using it; the sd for every column, under which a 0/1 treatment's prior depends on the treated share; arm's rule; and centring inside the package, which gives controls a term when zero means none. The cost is that the units of w are part of the model: a dose in grams and in milligrams are different priors, and a column far from zero mixes poorly until the caller centres it. A basis written with scale(), poly() or the like must predict at new rows from the training rows' centre and scale, as in lm; on the branch it was re-evaluated on the new rows, and that is fixed with this. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "Yes, do that. The package neither centres nor scales, the help says the sd is per unit of w and shows how to standardize, and the scale() defect gets fixed." See also: [dec-B253], [dec-B246].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args; TODO basis-formula-terms. Marked: revised by the maintainer, 2026-10-06: the default sd follows the column's standard deviation under dec-B261; a stated sd, no centring and no change to the column stand. [dec-B257]

**Every forest with a multiplier takes the same default sd, sqrt(2 / K)**
A forest that multiplies a numeric column and states no sd takes the default a factor multiplier already had, sqrt(2 / K) on the response's scale, K the number of forests, so a 0/1 treatment written as a number and the same treatment written as a factor get the same default effect: sd(y) in the two-forest causal model, bcf's default. The alternative was sqrt(1 / K) for a numeric column, 0.71 sd(y) in that model, stochtree's default, which had been argued for as keeping the prior sd of the sum of all terms at a row fixed; with the column used as written (dec-B257) that total depends on the column's units and the argument no longer holds. Read from their sources on 2026-10-05, the default sd of the coefficient function in packages that use the multiplier as given: bcf 2.0.2 sd(y), stochtree 0.4.5 0.71 sd(y), SoftBart 1.0.3 0.5 sd(y). Run the same day at 1.00 and 0.71 on the response's scale: under a gaussian response default fits did not differ beyond noise; under probit the larger was better by 0.009 in slope error with a real effect and worse by 0.015 with none. The cost is none found beyond that. The maintainer on 2026-10-05: "The factor's default for every multiplier, sqrt(2 / K)." See also: [dec-B257], [dec-B253].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: revised by the maintainer, 2026-10-06: the default is that size per standard deviation of the column under dec-B261, so a 0/1 number and the factor no longer share a default. [dec-B258]

**bartCause's p.weight standard error is in the response's units**
bartCause's p.weight method, and the built-in targeted estimator it falls back on when the tmle package is absent, rescale the response to the unit interval, convert each draw's estimate back to the response's units and had left its standard error on the unit interval, in released 1.0-10 as on the branch; summary() adds the squared standard error to the variance of the estimates over draws, so with a response whose range is far from one the reported sd was the posterior sd alone. The standard error is now multiplied by the response's range, as the estimate is. The tmle package returns its standard error in the response's units, and bartCause's own tmle path, which combines it by the same formula, already had it so. Run on 2026-10-05, 200 data sets at 300 and at 1000 rows, a response of range about 38, the effect on the treated, a nominal 95 percent interval: as released the interval covered the population effect 0.74 and 0.73 of the time and the two ways of computing the sd agreed to every digit; converted, 0.87 and 0.93, and the sample effect 0.95 and 0.97; plain BART's population interval 0.85 and 0.88. What still falls short is bias, the p.weight estimate being off by -0.34 and -0.15 in that design, about twice plain BART's. The alternative was leaving it, the intervals staying as released. The cost is that every p.weight interval is about 40 to 50 percent wider than in 1.0-10, which NEWS reports as a fix. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Convert the standard error to the response's units." See also: [dec-B244], [dec-B245].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B259]

**A forest's multiplier is a factor by its class, never by its content**
A forest's sd is the prior size of the effect of one step in its multiplier: one unit of a numeric column, each column's coefficient being N(0, 1), or a switch between two levels of a factor, each level's coefficient being N(0, 1/2). Which of the two a multiplier is follows its class, as in lm: a factor, a character vector or a logical vector is a factor, and any numeric vector or matrix is numbers, an indicator block included. The alternative, in the forest-prior design as critiqued, read a numeric matrix with exactly one nonzero entry in every row, all of one value, as a factor, so that bartCause's bcf, which passes cbind(1 - z, z), kept the factor's law without a change. Run by the design's critic, that rule follows the rows: two dummies with a level omitted are numbers, and a factor once a subset or a resample leaves no row at the omitted level (11 of 200 bootstrap resamples of a basis with 3 of 120 rows there); it turns on exact arithmetic (cbind(1 - z, z) * 0.1 * 3 a factor, cbind((1 - z) * 0.3, z * (0.1 * 3)) numbers); and one object had two kinds by entry point, dbartsData(bases = ) refusing a factor. lm, mgcv's s(x, by = ) and brms's gp(by = ) decide by class and never inspect content; stochtree and Hahn's bcf test a single treatment column for exactly 0 and 1 and nothing wider. The cost is that cbind(1 - z, z) given as numbers is two numeric coefficients, a level difference of 1.41 sd where factor(z) gives sd, which the help says; dbartsData(bases = ) takes a factor; bcf passes factor(z), its default fit unchanged bit for bit; and about 17 test swaps of a bare matrix onto a factor forest are rewritten. Run on the tip on 2026-10-06, before any of this is built: factor(z), z == 1 and cbind(1 - z, z) are one fit bit for bit and a 0/1 number another. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Class alone is fine." See also: [dec-B257], [dec-B258].
Record: this register. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B260]

**The default sd of a forest multiplying a numeric column is per standard deviation of the column; a stated sd is literal; the coefficient stays normal**
Under dec-B257 a numeric multiplier was used as written with a default sd per unit of the column, and a prototype built to it gave a zero estimate from a well-mixed chain, with no sign, once the column's spread fell below about a tenth of a unit (slope error 0.40 and a 90 percent interval covering 0.17 of the time at a spread of 0.01, against 0.18 and 0.90 at a spread of 1). The maintainer, shown that: "you've convinced me that this is going to be a problem. Let's take a step back and figure out what is actually good design here." Eight panel voices answering one brief apart from each other, a survey checked in primary sources, 8760 prototype fits, a designer's three candidates and a critic's 1140 further fits followed on 2026-10-06. The rule: when no sd is stated, the forest's default is sqrt(2 / K) on the response's scale divided by the standard deviation of the column, each column of a basis by its own, computed once when the sampler is created and kept with the data as a derived quantity; a stated sd is literal, per unit of the column as given; the column itself is never centred or divided; and the drawn coefficient keeps the normal law a factor's has. A default fit is then the same fit in any units, at 100 rows as at 500 and under probit as under gaussian. Read from their sources: rstanarm scales its default priors by the predictor's standard deviation and takes a stated prior as written, VCBART, flexBART and glmnet scale by it, on the ground that a slope per standard deviation of the predictor cannot much exceed the response's; all of them also centre, so this is that convention's scale without its centring; the rule of two standard deviations with a special case for two-valued columns was dropped by rstanarm in 2017 and 2020. On the coefficient the maintainer asked "do people really expect heavy-tailed distributions as a default?" They do not: no panel voice read a tail into sd before being shown the failure; bcf puts a half-normal on its treatment forest's scale by choice ("we prefer stronger regularization"), the one BART paper with a continuous multiplier does the same, and the guidance on scale priors takes a half-normal when the scaling is known, which the default now is. Measured, a half-Cauchy and the normal coefficient did not differ at ordinary units or with no slope, the normal was better under probit by 0.007 in slope error and 0.014 in coverage, and prior mass on probabilities beyond 0.01 and 0.99 was 0.27 against 0.31. The alternatives were the column as written with a half-Cauchy coefficient, nothing taken from the data, which failed at 100 rows (0.39, 0.51) and under probit (0.28, 0.74); the same scale with a half-Cauchy coefficient, the critic's and the orchestrator's first recommendation; and centring and scaling inside the package, the only one that fits a column far from zero, which changes what the first forest means and which no panel voice wanted. The cost is that the default prior depends on the data and, for a 0/1 treatment written as a number, on the treated share, its default effect 2, 2.5 and 4.6 times the factor's at 50, 20 and 5 percent treated though the fits match the factor's; that a single wild value inflates the scale and the normal coefficient cannot recover (one 1000 among 500 rows: 0.34, 0.36, where the half-Cauchy gave 0.21, 0.81), the printed scale being the only sign; that a stated sd in the wrong units is still a zero estimate; and that a column far from zero still fits badly until the caller centres it. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Scale the default and keep normal coefficient". See also: [dec-B257], [dec-B258], [dec-B253], [dec-B260].
Record: this register; TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B261]

**A caller may opt a drawn coefficient into a heavy-tailed law; built after the merge to main**
The law of a forest's drawn coefficient is not a caller's argument under dec-B253 and the default for a multiplied forest is normal (dec-B261). The maintainer, told that, asked "are these user-settable? Can they opt-into heavy tailed distributions?" The opt-in is one argument on forest(), its spelling still to be decided, under which the coefficient's size is half-Cauchy with the same prior median; the default does not change. Its user is a caller who runs the sampler inside a larger one and redraws the multiplier, whose scale drifts during the run: measured on 2026-10-06, a column replaced by one 30 times smaller under a normal coefficient gave slope error 0.27 with a 90 percent interval covering 0.50 of the time, against 0.18 and 0.88 under the half-Cauchy, and a stated sd 100 times too small 0.40 and 0.17 against 0.17 and 0.90. The alternative was leaving the law fixed, as bcf does. The cost is one more argument and about 300 lines with the reader's label and an exact test. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Sure, opt-in for 1.0. Post main-merge." See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B253].
Record: TODO coefficient-law-opt-in. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B262]

**A 0/1 treatment written as a number is scaled as any number, and the help says so**
Under dec-B261 a numeric column's default sd is per standard deviation of the column, and a 0/1 column's standard deviation follows the treated share (0.5 at half treated, 0.4 at 20 percent, 0.22 at 5 percent), so the default prior on the treatment effect of a 0/1 number is 2, 2.5 and 4.6 times the one factor(z) gets. No exception is made: a number is a number (dec-B260), the help for forest says that a treatment belongs in a factor or a logical, where sd is the treatment effect, and gives the figures, and the fit prints the default it resolved. Measured on 2026-10-06 at those three shares, the number against the factor: effect error 0.282, 0.332, 0.449 against 0.293, 0.335, 0.449; a 90 percent interval covering 0.83 to 0.85 against 0.76 to 0.80; with no effect, estimates of size 0.072, 0.102, 0.195 against 0.072, 0.093, 0.181; and the factor mixing two to six times slower. rstanarm since 2020, VCBART and flexBART scale a binary column by its standard deviation as any other; arm uses the gap between the two values, a rule rstanarm had and removed. The alternatives were that gap for a two-valued column, which decides by content and under which one stray third value moved the prior 2 to 7 times, and two standard deviations for every column, which matches the factor at half treated only, which no package uses without the two-valued case, and which covered worse under probit (0.83 against 0.87). The cost is that a user who writes a treatment as a 0/1 number and does not read the printed prior has a wider prior on the effect than bcf's convention, more so the rarer the treatment. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Leave it and document it." See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B260], [dec-B258].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B263]

**A numeric multiplier far from zero is the caller's to centre: no warning and no centring inside the package**
In y = f1(x) + w f2(x) the first forest is the response at w = 0, so a column that never comes near zero, an age or a year, makes that forest an extrapolation and the term w f2 mostly a second copy of it. Measured on 2026-10-06, slope error and the coverage of a 90 percent interval: 0.17 and 0.90 centred; 0.22 and 0.66 at 3 standard deviations from zero; 0.35 and 0.22 at 10; under probit 0.30 and 0.54 at 3, with no sign from the chain. The package does nothing to the column and raises nothing: the help for a numeric multiplier leads with the centred form, forest(basis = ~ scale(w)) or I(w - c), for a column that is an ordinary covariate, says why in a sentence (the first forest's default is placed on the observed response, and uncentred it must describe the response at zero), and the fit prints the column's mean and standard deviation beside the default it resolved; scale() in a basis predicts from the training rows (dec-B257). The orchestrator had recommended a warning when zero lies outside the column's range; the maintainer asked "Is the warning disproportionate? Should those users be centering, just for statistical purposes? Should try and silently fit a (roughly) equivalent model for them?" A warning suits a call that is probably a mistake and these are ordinary inputs, so it was withdrawn. Centring is the right thing statistically, the same model under the prior the defaults are built for where an unmultiplied forest covers the same columns; rstanarm and brms centre linear terms inside and report the intercept in the original terms, VCBART and flexBART centre. The package does not, as lme4 does not centre a random slope's predictor nor mgcv a numeric by: a default centre changes what a stated prior on the first forest means, from the response at zero to the response at the mean, for a dose or a treatment where zero means none; it cannot be applied only where zero is outside the data without deciding by content (dec-B260); a caller who redraws the multiplier would get a centre taken from starting values; and all eight panel voices called centring the user's job. Asked why the recommendation was "for 1.0" only, the orchestrator withdrew the qualifier: a default cannot change after a release without changing fits, so this is the design and anything later is an addition a caller asks for. The cost is that a user who multiplies by an uncentred covariate and reads neither the help nor the printed prior gets a poor slope with nothing to flag it. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "OK, then use your recommendation." See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B257].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B264]

**A replaced multiplier keeps its derived default sd unless updateBasisScale asks for a new one**
The default sd of a forest that multiplies a numeric column is derived from the column's standard deviation when the sampler is created and kept with the data (dec-B261). When the column is replaced through setForestBasis the default is kept, as setPredictor keeps the split points and setResponse the response scale (dec-B231: derived data changes only when the caller is explicit), and an argument, updateBasisScale, FALSE by default, re-derives it from the new column when TRUE. The update restates that forest's prior for the next sweep and leaves its trees as they are, as setLeafPrior does, a new column being no rescaling of the old one; like updateScale it is for burn-in only, a prior that moves afterwards making draws across sweeps no longer comparable. TRUE on a forest whose sd was stated is refused by name, there being no default to update; a constant column is refused wherever a default would have to be derived from it, at creation with no stated sd and on an update; and setData follows dec-B231, deriving when the new data object carries no value, keeping one it carries, an explicit updateBasisScale overriding both. The name is its own because updateScale on setData already means the response range: one argument per part of the sampler, as updateCutPoints, updateScale and updateStandardization (dec-B233). The orchestrator had recommended keeping the default with no argument, the caller restating sd through setLeafPrior, and the design's critic refusing the first replacement until sd was stated; the maintainer asked "Should this be treated like the response scale, where users can have it update during burn-in and be fixed after, using a flag?", which is the package's own idiom and answers what keeping alone left open, a caller whose starting column is arbitrary. The other alternative, recomputing on every replacement, is what the branch did: measured, the coefficient's prior sd moved from 0.77 to 0.25 between a column and three times it inside one chain, and every panel voice asked ruled it out. The cost is one more argument, and that a caller who never states sd and never updates samples under a default set by the first column: a column that later shrinks 30-fold gave slope error 0.27 and a 90 percent interval covering 0.50 of the time, against 0.18 and 0.88 at the right scale. No other package lets a multiplier be replaced during a run. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, asked to confirm the rule and the name: "Yes and updateBasisScale." See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B231], [dec-B233], [dec-B254].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B265]

**A forest states its sd as a plain argument, forest(sd = ), with leaf.prior = normal(sd = ) accepted as the long form**
dec-B246 gave forest() the front door's prior arguments, so a forest's spread was written forest(x1 + x2, basis = ~ dose, leaf.prior = normal(sd = 30)). Shown full calls in that form, with a several-column basis and one sd per column, the maintainer said: "Those are incredibly hard to parse. Can you design a cleaner form that places the sd closer to its basis? If we need to workshop this, maybe we should." A workshop on 2026-10-06 put four notations for the same five models to eight readers who saw only the calls and to four design voices: the form as designed; a plain sd inside the term; mgcv's forest(x, by = dose, sd = 30); and priors kept apart from the formula and matched by label. All four voices chose a plain sd inside forest(), in the response's units, with the long form still accepted so that a prior read from a sampler can be handed back and a later law has a place; the readers found no notation clearly easier, every one asked what the sd was the sd of, and none could read cgm(3, 0.25) without its argument names. The rule: forest(sd = 30) is the spelling the help shows; it is the scale of that forest's leaf prior, the quantity leaf.prior = normal(sd = 30) states, so the two are one statement, the maintainer having asked "Is it part of the leaf.prior?"; forest() accepts either and refuses both together by name; the reader and the writer on a live sampler trade normal() objects as dec-B246 ruled, in one unit. In a model of several forests the only leaf prior a forest can take is a normal with an sd, so the wrapper carried one number. The alternative was dec-B246 as it stood, one spelling and longer. The cost is two spellings of one statement, and that anything beyond the one number, a coefficient held or heavy-tailed among them, has to go in the long form. Examples name cgm's arguments, and the fit prints each sd both per unit and per standard deviation of its column, the slip every voice named being a stated sd left unchanged when its column is rescaled. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "OK, option A is fine." Revises dec-B246 in part. See also: [dec-B246], [dec-B253], [dec-B261].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B266]

**A multiplied forest is written one way, forest(x, basis = ~ w); the colon form goes**
A formula could state a forest's multiplier two ways: w:forest(x1 + x2), which takes only a bare name or factor(name) and sends anything else to the other form by its refusal, and forest(x1 + x2, basis = ~ w), which takes any expression and is also how the list interface writes it. The colon form is removed: a multiplier is the forest's basis argument, in a formula and in a list alike, and the colon is refused with a message naming the form to use. In the workshop of 2026-10-06 both readers shown a call that used the two spellings side by side stumbled on there being two, four of the six readers who saw the colon read it as an interaction, as it is in lm, and of the six who were not shown a multiplier inside the call five proposed one unprompted; the design voices split two against two, the two for the colon liking that it reads as z times tau(x). The argument keeps the package's word, basis, which setForestBasis already uses, over mgcv's by, under which a factor promises one function per level, which this model is not. The alternative was keeping both. The cost is the shorter spelling for the commonest case, and that forest(), new in 1.0-0, has its tests, help and examples respelled before the release; no released call changes. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Drop the colon. I also like z times tua(x), but colons don't directly read as that." See also: [dec-B266], [dec-B260].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B267]

**The first forest of a formula may be written as forest(x1 + x2)**
In a fitting function's formula the plain terms are the first forest, and y ~ forest(x1 + x2) + forest(x1 + x2, basis = ~ dose) is refused ("a formula whose only right-hand-side content is a forest() term has no predictors left for the base forest"). That form is now accepted: a forest() term with no basis is a forest with no multiplier, so each forest() in a formula is one function, and the multi-forest examples in the help are written that way. y ~ x1 + x2 + forest(x1 + x2, basis = ~ dose) keeps working, the plain terms being the first forest as they are for a single-forest fit. In the workshop of 2026-10-06, five of the eight readers shown only the calls took the plain x1 + x2 beside a forest() term for linear terms, the worst misreading found, in every notation tried, and three of them proposed writing the first forest out. The alternative was leaving the refusal. The cost is a second way to write the first forest; what a formula holding both plain terms and a forest() with no basis means is for the design to settle. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Option A." See also: [dec-B267], [dec-B241].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B268]

**A forest states one sd in 1.0; a longer sd is refused, and an unnamed vector for good**
A forest multiplied by several columns, one shared function times a weighted sum of them, has a default per column, each by its own standard deviation (dec-B261), and takes one stated sd, that many response units per unit of every column. In 1.0-0 sd is one unnamed number: an sd of length greater than one is refused by name, named or not, and a name on a single number is refused too, so that stating a size per column can be added later without changing what any 1.0 call means; an unnamed vector stays refused then, since reordering the columns would reassign its numbers with no sign. The form of the later per-column statement is not settled: a vector named by column, sd = c(dose = 30, age = 0.05), which all four of the second workshop round's voices chose over a positional one, or a wrapper on the basis term, which the maintainer is considering ("I still dislike how basis and sd are separated"). What the release fixes now for either: how a basis's columns are named, and a reader, extract and print that are per column, the default already resolving to one number per column. In that round, on 2026-10-06, all six readers shown only the calls read a two-column basis as two separate functions, whatever the form of the sd; naming the vector made them surer which number went with which column (70 and 75 percent against 65 and 70) and no surer what the model was. The alternative was accepting the named vector in 1.0, which two voices preferred because a reader that returns a default forest's two numbers should be able to hand them back; the reader instead returns what was stated, the default as the default, and shows the resolved numbers beside it. The cost is that a caller with columns in different units who wants to state sizes scales the columns in the basis, which is the same model and reads badly. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Option A is fine". See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B266].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06; revised in part by dec-B280 and dec-B282. [dec-B269]

**A basis is written as the right-hand side of a model formula, with no tilde**
A forest's basis was a one-sided formula whose right-hand side was evaluated as R code, so basis = ~ dose + age was one column, the sum, two columns needed cbind(dose, age), and the package carried its own code to rebuild scale() and poly() at new rows as lm would (dec-B257, dec-A162). The basis is now the right-hand side of a model formula, written without the tilde, as a forest's covariates already are: forest(x1 + x2, basis = dose + age) is two columns, I(dose + age) their sum, factor(z) one column per level, poly(dose, 2) and scale(age) what they are in lm, at predict too, through R's own model frame. forest() takes the argument unevaluated and finds its names in the data and then where the call was made, as lm does. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, of the cbind form: "I also dislike cbind - what else would a `+` mean in that context?", and of the tilde: "will anything ever be on the left-hand-side of the `~`? If not, we should do some formula / language hacking so we can interpret whatever is given as the RHS of a formula." Nothing is planned for the left-hand side. In the workshop's second round all four voices had asked that + at the top of a basis be refused, a sum fitted in silence being the worst reading, so as to leave this meaning open; it is taken directly instead. Made by the orchestrator and not yet ruled on: a one-sided formula is still accepted, for a caller who builds the basis in a variable; and what a basis's intercept, an interaction of two basis terms and a basis mixing a factor with numbers mean is for the design. The cost is that a basis written for the old reading with + changes meaning, none having been released, and that forest() evaluates one of its arguments in the caller's data, as subset() and the fitting functions do. See also: [dec-B267], [dec-B268], [dec-B260], [dec-B257], [dec-A162].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B270]

**A forest's coefficient law is one argument, amplitude; fixed() holds the coefficient, and update.amplitude goes**
A forest's coefficient was held with forest(update.amplitude = FALSE), and the opt-in heavy-tailed law of dec-B262 was to be a further argument whose spelling was undecided. The two are one statement, the law of the forest's coefficient, and forest() takes it as one argument: amplitude = fixed() holds the coefficient at the value its shape has (dec-B253), nothing stated is the normal law, drawn, as now, and the heavy-tailed law is added after the merge to main as another value of the same argument, amplitude = student(3) in the form shown to the maintainer. It is stated once for a forest and covers all of that forest's coefficients, so a basis's terms stay bare; update.amplitude is removed before the release, since afterwards it would stay as a second spelling, and bartCause's bcf, which passes it, changes one line. The maintainer had said of a wrapper on each basis term, normal(dose, sd = 30), "you have to put `normal` everywhere", and then "if student distributions are planned we'll need a way to differentiate"; this differentiates without a wrapper. All four voices of the workshop's second round asked that the hold and the heavy tail be two values of one argument and that update.amplitude be folded in before 1.0-0; they put it inside the prior object, leaf.prior = normal(sd = , amplitude = ), where the orchestrator proposed a plain argument beside sd, as dec-B266 made sd. fixed() is the constructor the package already uses for a value that is not drawn.
