The alternative was keeping update.amplitude and adding a second argument for the heavy tail. The cost is a rename of an argument new in 1.0-0, with its tests and help. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, shown the two options: "I like fixed()". See also: [dec-B262], [dec-B253], [dec-B254], [dec-B266].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args; TODO coefficient-law-opt-in. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B271]

**How a size per column is stated is decided after 1.0, on condition that it can be added without a breaking change**
Three ways of stating a different size for each column of a several-column basis are on the table and none is chosen: a vector named by column, sd = c(dose = 30, age = 0.05); a wrapper on the basis term, normal(dose, sd = 30) + normal(age, sd = 0.05), of which the maintainer said "you have to put `normal` everywhere"; and a unit on the term, basis = dose / 30 + age / 10, which the maintainer floated. A reading test on 2026-10-06, eight readers shown only the calls and asked what one more unit of dose does to y: both readers of the named vector gave the right figures, one of the two shown the wrapper did with a guess, and none of the four shown the division did outright, taking it for a rescaling of the data, which three of them said they would do before the call. The choice waits until after the release, the maintainer's condition being that it must not then need a breaking change: "It can wait as long as we're confident that we're not likely to have to introduce a breaking change to support it." What 1.0-0 does so that each of the three is an addition, changing the meaning of no call it accepts: an sd of length greater than one and a name on a single sd are refused by name (dec-B269); a basis term that calls a prior constructor is refused by name; a term divided or multiplied by a number at the top of a basis is refused by name, as R's own formula code refuses it today ("invalid model formula"), while I(dose / 30) is accepted as the rescaled column it is and would mean the same under the third form; the names of a basis's columns are fixed, as coef() would show them; one sd on several columns means that many response units per unit of each, now and later; and the reader, extract and print are per column from the start. The engine holds one scale per column from the slice that builds the default of dec-B261, which lands before the release and must keep it; the tip does not yet, the design's prototype does. With that in place no stored state or compiled interface changes with any of the three. What would break a 1.0 call is changing what one sd on several columns means, or how columns are named; nothing else was found, and the critic of the surface design is asked to try to build a counterexample. The alternative was choosing now. See also: [dec-B269], [dec-B270], [dec-B271], [dec-B266].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B272]

**A star in a basis is refused in 1.0**
With a basis read as the right-hand side of a model formula (dec-B270), basis = dose * age would be lm's three columns, dose, age and their product, where the same text on the tip is one column, the product, so the reading would change with no sign; and a star by a number is one of the spellings a later unit on the term would use (dec-B272). In 1.0-0 a star at the top of a basis is refused by name; the product is written I(dose * age) and the three columns dose + age + I(dose * age). The alternative was lm's meaning. The cost is that a caller who expects a model formula's crossing writes it out. The design's critic, asked on 2026-10-06 what in the accepted grammar could force a breaking change later, named this as the one operator to settle now. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Refuse * for now." See also: [dec-B270], [dec-B272].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B273]

**A forest's default tree count and tree prior go by its kind, not by where it is written**
The first forest of a model takes the fit's own tree count and tree prior, and a later one the multiplied forest's defaults, fewer and shallower trees, so in a formula whose forests all have a basis, y ~ forest(x1, basis = a) + forest(x2, basis = b), whichever is written first is treated as the plain one and swapping the two terms is another model (run by the design's critic on 2026-10-06: 20 trees of 4.0 nodes each against 50 of 1.5). The defaults now go by kind, as a forest's default sd already does (dec-B258, dec-B261): a forest with no basis takes the usual defaults and the fitting function's n.trees and tree.prior are its; a forest with a basis takes the multiplied forest's defaults wherever it is written, in a formula and in a list alike. Where no forest is without a basis, a tree count or tree prior given to the fitting function has no forest to belong to, 100 each and 100 in all being equally likely readings, and that argument alone is refused by name, with a message saying to state it on a forest; the model is accepted. The orchestrator and the critic had recommended refusing such a formula in 1.0; the maintainer: "I think A would confuse users since it would be an unexpected failure of an input that seems like it should work. What is going to match a users intent here?" What matches it is that two forests of one kind are treated alike and that addition commutes. The other alternative was written order, as built. The cost is that a fit which puts a multiplied forest first and states no tree count or tree prior changes its defaults, no such call having been released; that the rule of dec-B241, the first forest's count being the fit's, becomes the plain forest's; and that it is not built. bartCause's bcf is not affected, its first forest having no basis. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Use B then." See also: [dec-B241], [dec-B246], [dec-B268].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B274]

**extract(type = "leaf.prior.sd") returns a list by forest of vectors named by column**
For a model of several forests, extract(fit, type = "leaf.prior.sd") returns a list with one entry for each forest, named by the forest's label, each entry a numeric vector with one number for each column of that forest's basis, named as the column is, and a single unnamed number for a forest with no basis; the numbers are the sd in force, stated or resolved from the default, in the response's units per unit of the column. A default already resolves to a different number for each column (dec-B261), so one number for a forest could not describe a several-column basis, and the shape is fixed for good, since a size stated per column later (dec-B272) has to come back in the same form. The alternative was one flat named vector with names joining the forest and the column, dose.dose and dose.age, which puts every number in one vector and makes a caller split names to find a forest's own; the orchestrator's first proposal, before the default was per column, had been that flat vector. The cost is that the result for an ordinary fit of one-column forests is a list of length-one vectors. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, shown the two: "A." See also: [dec-B269], [dec-B272], [dec-B253].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B275]

**Selecting a forest by its label is built before the merge to main; updateBasisScale and the long form on forest() after it**
Three pieces of the forest surface are additions that nothing else waits on, about 2400 of the 21300 lines planned for the arc: setForestBasis(updateBasisScale = TRUE), which re-derives a default sd during burn-in (dec-B265); the long form leaf.prior = normal(sd = ) on forest() (dec-B266); and selecting a forest by its label, as getLeafPrior("dose"). Selecting by label is built before the merge to main: the reader, extract and print are to name forests by label (dec-B275; at the tip none does yet, as the plan for this slice found), and a forest that can be named and not asked for would be odd. The other two are built after the merge to main, for 1.0-0 still, as the heavy-tailed coefficient law is (dec-B262): until then a caller who replaces a column whose forest is at the default sd states the sd, and a caller writes sd = and not the long form, which is refused as R refuses an argument a function does not have, so that accepting it later changes no call. Run by the design's critic on 2026-10-06: leaving any of the three out breaks nothing later. The alternatives were building all three before the merge and deferring all three. The cost is that a prior read from a sampler, a normal() object, cannot be handed to forest() until the long form lands, only to setLeafPrior; and that a default-sd forest whose column is replaced keeps the first column's default with no way to refresh it but stating one. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, of keeping the label and deferring the other two: "Sure, option C. For deferring, just until it lands on main." See also: [dec-B262], [dec-B265], [dec-B266], [dec-B272], [dec-B275].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args; TODO forest-after-merge. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B276]

**A saved state names its zero-weight rows, so a Student-t restore under other weights redraws only the rows that enter**
A Student-t fit holds a latent scale for every row, and a row at weight zero keeps one drawn without that row's residual. A state stores the scales and a digest of the weights it was stored under, not which rows were at zero, so a state installed under other weights, by setState, a copy or a reload, could not tell a stale scale from a live one and redrew the scale of every row in the likelihood (dec-A160); the exact route was to put the stored weights back first and restore then, an order the manual gave. The state now also records which rows were at weight zero when it was stored, and an install under other weights redraws the scale of exactly the rows that enter the likelihood, those at zero then and positive now, as the same setWeights call would; an install under its own weights draws nothing, as before, and a state stored before this, which names no rows, is installed as it was, every scale in the likelihood redrawn where it carries a digest and none where it does not. The orchestrator had recommended leaving it, the case being rare and an exact order documented; the maintainer: "Option B. A sounds hostile to the user." The alternative was that. The cost is about 480 lines by the plan's estimate, tests included, and a state wider by one byte for each row of every Student-t fit, weighted or not, since an unweighted fit's state installed under weights with zeros had every scale redrawn too; not built. With setState first and the old weights second the sampler is right in law, and the stored chain bit for bit only when the change moved no row into or out of the likelihood; with the weights put back first it is bit for bit always. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, as quoted. Revises dec-A160 in part. See also: [dec-A160], [dec-B238], [dec-B234].
Record: docs/plans/state-zero-weight-rows.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B277]

**A monotone sampler resets a tree only on a forced update; an unforced one that would break a tree is refused**
New predictor values can leave a monotone tree's leaf values out of order along the constrained predictor: a forced update empties leaves and merges them beside new neighbours, and a column that first gains missing values gains a position that relates leaves the order did not. As built (dec-A129, dec-A131) such a tree had every leaf set to zero, drawing nothing, on both routes, so an unforced setPredictor that brought a column its first missing value was accepted and reset the tree, the one unforced update that changed a fit it did not refuse; an unforced update that would empty a leaf was already refused. The maintainer was given the reset, a refusal on a monotone sampler, and the reset with a warning, for the forced update: "forceUpdate is supposed to \"figure it out\", and is decent during burn-in. Without it, it should reject if it would leave the sampler in an incoherent state, as it does today." So a forced update keeps the silent reset, as do setData, setCutPoints and a warm start, which take no such flag and always complete (the orchestrator's reading), and an unforced update, whole-matrix, by column or row by row, that would leave a tree out of order is refused as one that empties a leaf is, the sampler left as it was; the help says both. In 24 trial forced updates on 400 rows, a free column redrawn for a quarter or all of the rows or flattened, at 10, 50 and 200 trees, no tree was reset. The unforced refusal is not built; about 450 lines by its plan. The plan found the one such update narrower than "a column": only an unordered factor column's first missing value relates leaves anew, a numeric or ordered column's going left at every rule (no pair added in 20000 random trees), and from R it arrives through the whole-matrix form given codes and the joint row form, the column forms refusing a factor's first missing value by name already; a setState that merges emptied leaves resets as the forced calls do. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, as quoted. Revises dec-A129 and dec-A131 in part. See also: [dec-A129], [dec-A131].
Record: docs/plans/monotone-unforced-refusal.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B278]

**The joint row-by-row update is fixed for a factor before the merge to main**
Planning dec-B278 turned up a defect outside it: updatePredictorPerObservationJointly reads its values as the engine's category codes, from 0, and a factor handed to it arrives as R's codes, from 1, so every level is installed as the next one with no message (200 of 200 rows in the probe), or the call is refused as naming no existing category when the top level is present. The manual says the values are numeric there, so a factor is undocumented input that nothing refuses. Told of it, the maintainer: "Let's fix the updatePredictorPerObservationJointly bug pre-merge." No alternative was put. What the fix does with a factor was left to the slice's plan, which matches its labels to the column's levels with the helper setPredictor by column uses and keeps numbers as the codes they are today; the plan also found a character vector installed as missing values (199 of 200 rows). The maintainer on 2026-10-06, as quoted.
Record: docs/plans/factor-column-update-forms.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-06. [dec-B279]

**A named single sd is taken on a forest whose basis has one column or none**
dec-B269 refuses a name on a single sd so that a size stated per column, sd = c(dose = 30, age = 0.05), can be added later without changing what a 1.0 call means. Ordinary R trips on it: a number indexed out of a named vector keeps its name, so forest(x, basis = ~ dose, sd = pars["k"] / 2) is refused with a hint to unname() it, two of the package's own vignettes passed such a number, base R ignores the name everywhere (rnorm(1, sd = pars["k"])) and so does the package's own k (run on 2026-10-07). The rule: a named single sd is accepted with its name ignored on a forest whose basis has one column or none, and stays refused by name on a basis of several columns, the one place where a name could later choose a column. The alternatives were keeping the refusal everywhere, which constrains nothing later and fails a valid-looking call with a fix few users have typed, and accepting it everywhere, under which sd = c(dose = 30) on a two-column basis means 30 for both columns for good and could never mean 30 for dose and the default for age. The cost is a rule with a condition, and that a later per-column form must ignore a single number's name on a one-column basis whatever the name is. Not built. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown the three: "Accept it unless the basis has several columns." Revises dec-B269 in part. See also: [dec-B269], [dec-B272], [dec-A171].
Record: root TODO, named-single-sd. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B280]

**A factor of three or more levels is refused as a basis in 1.0-0; a function for each level is scheduled as the later meaning**
A basis never makes more forests: a forest on a factor is one function with one coefficient for each level, b[g] f(x), so with three levels every level's effect against the first is a multiple of one curve. The shape exists because the engine holds a coefficient for each basis column, as BCF's two do, and not because anyone asked for it; its plausible users, ordered doses or sites sharing one effect pattern at different strengths, are few. Asked how a factor is interpreted and who would want one forest for all levels, the maintainer was shown that someone with three arms who writes forest(x, basis = ~ arm) expects an effect function for each arm, as all six workshop readers read a two-column basis, and gets one curve rescaled with nothing in the output to say so; grf's multi-arm forest and stochtree's BCF both fit a function for each arm, and no package found fits the shared shape. The rule: in 1.0-0 a factor, character or logical basis of three or more levels is refused by name, whether its coefficients are drawn or held, with a message that shows the form that works today, one forest for each level but the first, forest(x, basis = ~ arm == "b") + forest(x, basis = ~ arm == "c") (run on 2026-10-07). After the release forest(x, basis = ~ arm) is to become shorthand for exactly that. The alternative was accepting it as the shared shape, as the branch does, which closes the shorthand reading for good. The cost is that a call which looks valid fails until the shorthand is built, and that the shared-shape model has no spelling. A two-level factor is not affected. Not built. The maintainer on 2026-10-07: "Refuse for 1.0 but make sure the sugar form that gets forests for all levels is scheduled for later." See also: [dec-B260], [dec-B269], [dec-B271].
Record: root TODO, factor-basis-refusal and factor-basis-per-level. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B281]

**A basis of several numeric columns is refused in 1.0-0**
dec-B269 took a forest multiplied by several columns as one shared function times a weighted sum of them, f(x) (b1 dose + b2 age), and refused only a size stated for each column. Once a basis is read as a model formula (dec-B270), forest(x, basis = ~ dose + age) is that one forest of two columns, the call all six workshop readers took for two separate functions, and the reasoning of dec-B281 applies to it unchanged; a spline, basis = ~ ns(dose, 3), is the same mechanism and a more sensible model, a curve in dose times a modifier, g(dose) f(x) (both run on 2026-10-07 on the build under review). Asked whether to refuse both, to refuse several terms and keep one term of several columns, or to keep both, the maintainer: "I thought we agreed that multiple columns would be refused for 1.0." The record had refused several sds and not several columns; shown that, and asked whether the several-column basis itself is refused, the spline included, the maintainer: "Yes." The rule: in 1.0-0 a basis is one numeric column or a factor, character or logical of two levels (dec-B281); a basis of several numeric columns is refused by name, however it is written, with a message that shows one forest for each column, forest(x, basis = ~ dose) + forest(x, basis = ~ age). What several columns mean is decided after the release, shorthand for a forest each or the shared shape, and either is an addition. The alternatives were the two above. The cost is that the curve-times-modifier model has no spelling in 1.0-0 and a call that looks valid fails. With it, nothing a 1.0 user can write has more than one size for a forest: the condition of dec-B280 has no case left, a named single sd being taken on every forest, the question of what a held coefficient is on several numeric columns does not arise, and the per-column forms of dec-B269, dec-B272 and dec-B275 keep their shape for later with one number in each. Revises dec-B269 in part. Not built. See also: [dec-B281], [dec-B269], [dec-B270], [dec-B272], [dec-B275], [dec-B280].
Record: root TODO, several-column-basis-refusal and several-column-basis-meaning. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B282]

**setState does what it can with no guarantee: a state does not record its family**
dec-A174 had every state record the family that stored it, one string at its top level that is compared and never installed, and refused a state of another family by name, a gaussian one included. Asked to mark that entry, the maintainer asked whether the state itself was tagged with its family, was told it was, that it is the first thing in a state to name a model fact, and that the alternative refuses only what the stored values can show; and ruled: "I'd rather just let setState do what it can with no guarantees. If it make sense, it can do what other set functions do: force an update to a reasonable coherent state or accept/reject if it is obviously broken." Asked first whether the record was used anywhere: it is written when a state is stored and read by the one refusal, no other code reads it, no consumer installs a state across families, and it was never released. The rule: the record and the refusal by family are removed; setState, a copy and a reload install a state whose blocks fit the sampler, and refuse one whose shape does not fit or whose values are plainly broken, the floor of dec-A174 standing, a Student-t, logistic or negative-binomial sampler refusing scales or Polya-Gamma variates that are not positive and finite. Measured when the floor was planned, on states with the record removed: of 380 ordered pairs of twenty kinds of sampler 325 are refused, the 15 that left fits not finite or a sweep that did not return all among them, and 55 install and run. What such a pair then holds is not promised: a gaussian state installs into a probit sampler again, and 30 of the pairs that ran left the sampler holding another family's latent block as stored until its next sweep redraws it. The alternative was dec-A174 as built. The cost is that a probit state installed into a logistic sampler runs with no message. Revises dec-A174. See also: [dec-A174], [dec-B254], [dec-B234].
Record: docs/plans/state-family-record-removal.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B283]

**A forest's basis is read when forest() is called, a formula object included, and the object is left a working formula**
forest() is called before the fit, so a basis written in it is used later than it is written. The basis slice as built read a tilde typed inside the call on the spot and gave a formula made beforehand R's own rule, looked up when the model is fitted, so of two loops over c("dose", "age") the one writing forest(basis = ~ W[[k]]) gave its forests dose and age and the one writing f <- ~ W[[k]]; forest(basis = f) gave both age, with no message (run on 2026-10-07 on the build under review). That split was the orchestrator's call. Shown it with two alternatives, every basis read on the spot and every basis under R's rule, the maintainer: "I guess we can always read on the spot, but I think we want the formula object to behave correctly for other uses so whatever changes you do you might need to repair the object after." The rule: whatever form a basis takes, typed in place, a formula object or plain code, forest() reads it at the call; a name that is a column of the fit's data is still that column, read when the model is fitted, the data hiding a caller's variable of the same name as it does in any model formula. Reading leaves the caller's formula as it was, its environment and attributes untouched and nothing assigned where it looks, and a formula the forest keeps or hands back is an ordinary formula that other code can use. The cost is that a formula object given to forest() does not follow a variable changed afterwards, as it would under R's rule. A forest() term inside a model formula is read with that formula, at the fit, as before; $setForestBasis is not covered by this ruling. See also: [dec-B270], [dec-A173].
Record: docs/plans/written-surface.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B284]

**setCutPoints sorts its grid and refuses a repeated point; weights on cut points are left open for later**
dec-A170 had setCutPoints take a strictly increasing numeric grid, or the grid the column holds, and refuse the rest. Asked to mark it, the maintainer first chose an error over coercion for a grid that is not numeric ("we can error instead of coerce"), then asked "why not allow repeated points and why not sort the value vector?" Nothing argues against sorting: a grid is a set of thresholds, and base R's cut() sorts its breaks (run on 2026-10-07). A repeated point was refused because a stored split names its cut by value and a restore puts it on the first position holding that value, so the chain does not continue as stored (TODO repeated-cut-restore). The maintainer's use for one: "to make the sampler more likely to propose splits above and below those points." The engine picks uniformly among grid positions, so a point given twice is twice as likely, in the tree prior and the proposal both; there is no probability for a cut point otherwise, only for a column (split.probs), and a grid denser near a value is what serves the aim today. Shown three options, sort and refuse repeats in 1.0-0, sort and keep repeats as weights once the restore is fixed, and sort and drop repeats in silence, the maintainer: "I think we want to fix that defect already, right? That said, we can use option A." The rule: setCutPoints sorts the grid it is given; a grid with a repeated point is refused by name, the message pointing to a denser grid, unless it is the grid the column holds, which is kept exactly so a change can be undone; a grid that is not numeric is refused. Weighting a cut point, by repeats or by an argument of probabilities, is an addition for after the release that changes no 1.0 call, which dropping repeats in silence would have closed. The restore's defect is fixed before the merge on its own account, the sampler building grids with repeated points itself, over a constant column and a narrow one. The cost is that a repeat, harmless to the model, fails. Revises dec-A170 in part. See also: [dec-A170], [dec-B231].
Record: docs/plans/small-rulings-batch.md; root TODO, cut-point-weights and repeated-cut-restore. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07; revised in part by dec-B300. [dec-B285]

**The joint row update refuses numbers for a factor column**
dec-A172 left numbers given to updatePredictorPerObservationJointly for a factor column as what they had been, the engine's category codes counted from 0, on the ground that a caller moving a latent class inside a larger sampler holds codes, and had the help warn that as.integer() of a factor counts from 1. Asked to mark it, the maintainer was shown three readings: codes from 0, as built, under which a vector counted from 1 moves every level by one with no message unless the top level is present, the defect dec-B279 was ruled to fix; a refusal, labels or a factor only, which is what setPredictor on one column already does; and numbers counted from 1, which changes what existing calls mean. The orchestrator recommended the refusal and said its first call had been too cautious. The maintainer on 2026-10-07: "Refuse numbers for a factor column." The rule: for a factor column the joint form takes a factor or labels, matched to the column's levels by name, and refuses numbers by name, in the words setPredictor uses. The cost is that a caller holding codes converts them to labels on every call, and that calls passing codes ran before and are refused now; no consumer passes a factor column to it, bairrtt moving a numeric one (searched on 2026-10-07). Revises dec-A172 in part. See also: [dec-A172], [dec-B279].
Record: docs/plans/small-rulings-batch.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B286]

**A removal at the top of a formula takes from the plain predictors only and never reaches inside a forest()**
dec-A173 gave the forest with no basis the terms R gives the formula with every forest that has a basis deleted, so a removal written at the top, - x3, takes x3 from the plain predictors and leaves a forest() that names x3 as written, and a removal that names no plain term is passed by, as R passes one by. Run on the branch on 2026-10-07: y ~ . - x3 - z + forest(x3, basis = ~ factor(z)) fits x1 and x2 in the first forest and x3 in the second; y ~ x1 + x2 + forest(x3 + x1, basis = ~ factor(z)) - x1 fits x2 and then x1 and x3; and y ~ x1 + x2 + forest(x3, basis = ~ factor(z)) - x3 is the model without the removal. It is R's own rule for a formula, where y ~ x1 + s(x1) - x1 drops only the plain x1. The alternative was refusing a removal that names a column used inside a forest(), which would catch the last call, a removal that does nothing, and would refuse the second, a reasonable way of saying that x1 enters only through the second forest. The cost is that a removal that names nothing plain is passed by in silence. The maintainer on 2026-10-07: "As built. The intent is obvious: the first, implied forest uses predictors in the supplied data that aren't x3 and z, while the second forest is on x3 with z as a basis." See also: [dec-A173], [dec-B267], [dec-B268].
Record: docs/plans/written-surface.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B287]

**The words of monotone() abbreviate, directions and prior alike, by base R's matching, and the help does not call it out**
dec-A130 put the prior's name through match.arg, so prior = "l" is "leaf", while a direction word matched exactly, "inc" being refused (run on 2026-10-07). Asked to mark the difference, the maintainer: "I thought we based the vocabulary on what other packages accept." The directions are (dec-B147: gbm, xgboost and LightGBM take 1, -1 and 0, mboost's bmono takes words); the prior's two words have no outside vocabulary, no other package offering the choice. The orchestrator's note that mboost matches its words exactly was wrong: bmono puts its constraint through match.arg and takes "inc" (its source, read on 2026-10-07). Shown three options, both abbreviating, both exact, and the split as built, the maintainer: "Both abbreviate but using base R's mechanisms. We don't need to call it out in the docs if it is an eco-system wide standard." The rule: a direction word is matched as base R matches a choice, any unique abbreviation of "increasing" or "decreasing" taken, case-sensitively, by pmatch or match.arg and no matching code of the package's own; the numbers 1, -1 and 0 are as they were; the prior's name stays under match.arg; and the help lists the full words without a sentence on abbreviation. The alternatives were the two above. The cost is that "i" and "d" are directions. Revises dec-A130 in part. See also: [dec-A130], [dec-B147].
Record: docs/plans/small-rulings-batch.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B288]

**A factor basis that arrives with its levels in another order is matched to the fit's levels by name**
A forest's basis can be replaced during a run through $setForestBasis, and predict takes a basis for new rows. On the branch a factor given there is read by the position of its levels, so a fit made with levels control and treated, handed factor(z2, levels = c("treated", "control")), exchanges the two arms with no message; the second review of the basis slice found the swap putting one level's column under the other's name (2026-10-07). The plan for a forest's kind matches a swapped or predicted factor to the levels recorded at creation by name and refuses a level the fit never saw, as base R's predict matches a factor to the fit's levels by label and stops on a new one. The alternative was position, as built. The cost is that a caller who relied on position with other labels is refused. Not built. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown the two: "Match levels by name." See also: [dec-B260], [dec-B281].
Record: docs/plans/forest-kind-by-class.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B289]

**A held forest with no basis defaults to the size bart() gives its own forest**
In a model of several forests a forest with no basis whose coefficient is held, amplitude = fixed(), is an ordinary BART forest whose prior size is exactly its sd. With no sd stated the branch gives it sd(y) under a gaussian response, by a law that ignores sd, and the multiplier plan gave it 2 sd(y), the number the same forest has when its coefficient is drawn, where 2 sd(y) is the median of a heavy-tailed scale. The maintainer: "I don't see the motivation for 2 sd(y). Were there reasons cited for it, vs 1.4?" The record holds one, that it is bcf's number: bcf's sd_control defaults to 2 * sd(y), documented as "SD(mu(x)) marginally at any covariate value (or its prior median if use_muscale=TRUE)" (its source, read on 2026-10-07). As a median it is forgiving, the scale being drawn; held it is a fixed size with nothing to correct it, and nothing but bcf's use argues for it, where a quarter of the response's range, about 1.4 sd(y) for a normal sample of 300, is what bart() has given an ordinary forest throughout. The maintainer: "It's an option, right? And we're just setting the default? In that case, use option B. Presumably the bcf implementation in bartCause can plug in 2." The rule: under a gaussian response a held forest with no basis that states no sd takes the default bart() gives the one forest of an ordinary fit on the same response; a drawn one keeps 2 sd(y) as its median; a stated sd overrides either; and bartCause's bcf states 2 sd(y) on its first forest when update.a is FALSE, keeping bcf's convention. The alternatives were 2 sd(y), one number for the kind whether held or drawn, and sd(y), the branch's. The cost is that held and drawn defaults differ. The default under probit and logistic was not put and is not ruled. Not built. See also: [dec-B253], [dec-B261], [dec-B271].
Record: docs/plans/forest-multiplier.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B290]

**A forest's default sd does not depend on how many forests the model has**
A forest with a basis defaulted to sqrt(2 / K) on the response's scale in a model of K forests, so that declaring more of them did not widen the prior on their sum without bound. dec-B258 gave a numeric multiplier "the factor's default", which already carried K from earlier agent work; the dependence on K was never put to the maintainer. Asked "Does it make sense for the prior size to decrease with the number of forests?", the orchestrator gave both sides: it copies what BART does with trees, and matters most under probit and logistic, where the scale is fixed; but trees are interchangeable and forests are named effects, so the default prior on a treatment effect was 1.00, 0.82 and 0.71 of sd(y) with two, three and four forests only because unrelated terms were added, the forest with no basis did not shrink with K at all, and rstanarm, brms, mgcv and lme4 give each term its own prior however many there are. Measured on 2026-10-05 at 1.00 against 0.71, gaussian fits did not differ beyond noise and probit moved by about 0.01 either way. The maintainer on 2026-10-07: "Drop K, but is there some sort of convenient short-hand we could use to allow the user to scale it? For instance, with a BCF or factor, each forest is meant to consume different parts of the total variance, but with truly additive forests they're all working together. It might make sense to be able to easily switch on a mode where the forests are attempting to consume the total variance together, particularly as you noted for responses like logistic and probit." The rule: a forest with a basis that states no sd defaults to 1 on the response's scale, sd(y) under gaussian and the link's unit otherwise, per standard deviation of its column for a number (dec-B261), whatever the number of forests; at two forests that is the number it was, so the two-forest causal model keeps its prior. A way for forests to share one total is wanted and not designed (TODO forests-shared-total). The alternative was sqrt(2 / K) as built. The cost is that with many forests the prior on their sum grows, most felt under probit and logistic. Not built. Revises dec-B258 in part. See also: [dec-B258], [dec-B261], [dec-B253].
Record: docs/plans/forest-multiplier.md; root TODO, forests-shared-total. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B291]

**A model may hold several forests with no basis, sharing one total; built after the merge to main and before the release**
A model takes one forest with no basis: y ~ forest(x1) + forest(x2) + forest(x3) is refused ("a model has one forest with no multiplier, and every other forest states a 'basis'"), and forests that simply add are written with a basis column of ones (both run on 2026-10-07). The maintainer, dropping K from the default (dec-B291), asked for a short way to have forests "consume the total variance together", distinguishing a BCF or a factor, where "each forest is meant to consume different parts of the total variance", from "truly additive forests" that are "all working together". That distinction is the rule, with no switch: forests with no basis work on one quantity and share one prior total, each an equal part, as the trees of a forest do; a forest with a basis stands for its own effect and keeps its own prior. Shown three options, several forests with no basis after 1.0, the same before the merge, and one such forest with an explicit switch for forests with a basis to share a total, the maintainer: "Allow several plain forests, sharing one total. Post-merge, pre-1.0." The rule: y ~ forest(x1) + forest(x2) + forest(x3) is a model of three forests with no basis whose sum has the prior one such forest has; it is built after the merge to main and before the release, and until then several forests with no basis stay refused, so it is an addition. To design: how the engine tells such forests apart, which it does today by their multipliers; whose the fitting function's tree count and tree prior are (dec-B274 gives them to the forest with no basis); whether a stated sd on one of them is its own size or its share; and the default under probit and logistic. The alternative of a switch was not taken. See also: [dec-B291], [dec-B274], [dec-B253].
Record: root TODO, forests-shared-total. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B292]

**Under probit and logistic a held forest with no basis keeps a default of 1 in the link's unit**
dec-B290 gave a held forest with no basis bart()'s own size under a gaussian response and left probit and logistic open. There is no such size to copy there: bart()'s default for a binary response puts a prior on k, chi with 1.5 degrees of freedom and scale 2, so its forest's size is learned (run on 2026-10-07), and a held forest has a fixed size by definition. The plan keeps the branch's value, 1 in the link's own unit, chosen so that a two-forest probit model's prior puts the weight on probabilities below 0.01 or above 0.99 that an ordinary one-forest binary fit does (0.238 against 0.239, per the help). Asked whether any package of several forests goes through a link and what it does, the maintainer was shown, from their sources read the same day: stochtree's BCF with a probit outcome starts its prognostic forest at 1.41 and learns the scale by default, and fixes its treatment forest near 1.15, from a largest plausible effect on the probability of 0.9; flexBART under probit and logit gives every forest of a model of several 0.5, fixed, however many there are, and its one forest 1.0; bcf is gaussian only. The alternative was 1.5, what a fixed k of 2 would give, wider than any fixed size found and with no calibration behind it. The cost is none found; fits do not move. The maintainer on 2026-10-07: "OK, Keep 1." See also: [dec-B290], [dec-B291].
Record: docs/plans/forest-multiplier.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B293]

**setState installs what fits of a state whose store of kept trees is another size**
A sampler that keeps its draws' trees holds a store of control@n.samples of them, and setState refused a state stored by a sampler with another n.samples: a state of ten kept draws handed to a sampler of four gives "state is not consistent with this sampler", the sampler untouched (run on 2026-10-07). That was dec-A138's call, taken so that n.samples always describes the store the sampler has. Asked to mark it, the maintainer was shown three options: the refusal; adopting the state's size and rewriting n.samples, which changes a setting the caller chose; and installing what fits, the live chain going in and, of the state's kept draws, the most recent that the sampler's store holds, with the call reporting that the install was not exact, which is dec-B283's rule that setState does what it can. The maintainer on 2026-10-07: "Install what fits." The rule: a state whose kept store is larger or smaller than the sampler's installs; the chain's own trees and scalars are taken as from any state; the sampler's n.samples and store size do not change; where the state holds more kept draws than the store, the most recent are kept, and where fewer, all of them, the rest of the store empty; and setState returns what it returns for an install that is not exact. A reloaded sampler object still takes its store's size from its state, having no other record of it. The cost is that kept draws beyond the store's size are dropped with only the return value to say so, and engine work in the kept-draw store. Not built. Revises dec-A138 in part. See also: [dec-A138], [dec-B283], [dec-B254].
Record: root TODO, state-store-size-install. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07; revised in part by dec-B305. [dec-B294]

**A warm start takes all it can from the donor, collapsing a tree it cannot hold; installTrees refuses whole unless told to force**
A warm start, bart(warm.start = ) or the sampler's installTrees, builds every donor tree on scratch copies before it touches the sampler, so that a refused warm start leaves the sampler as it was; that was dec-A138's second call, with installing directly and rolling back as the alternative. Asked to confirm it, the maintainer went past the question: "Warm starts should try to get as much information out of the fit it can. If trees can be collapsed to be made into valid ones, that would make sense. Install trees might have modes that work a force flag, where if it was absent it would accept/reject." Run the same day: a donor fitted on 300 rows installs into a sampler on 40 of them, where many of its splits have a side no row reaches, as a restore of the same trees does with a return of FALSE; what a warm start refuses whole today is a donor of another tree count or DART setting, one whose trees split on a column the recipient's forest may not use, and one with malformed parameters. The rule: bart(warm.start = ) is best effort, a donor tree the recipient cannot hold as it is being collapsed at the split that offends, that split and everything beneath it becoming one leaf, as a forced setPredictor and a restore already collapse a split with an empty side, and the fit proceeding; installTrees takes a force argument, in the idiom of setPredictor's forceUpdate, without which it accepts the donor whole or refuses it whole with the sampler untouched, as built, and with which it collapses. A donor of another tree count is the same question's other half (dec-B247) and is designed with it. To design: the argument's name and default, what each form returns so a caller can tell an exact install from a collapsed one, what collapsing does to a leaf's value, and which mismatches stay refusals under force (another leaf model, another forest count). Not built. Revises dec-A138 in part. See also: [dec-A138], [dec-B247], [dec-B283], [dec-B294].
Record: root TODO, warm-start-salvage. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B295]

**What the response gives a prior is taken from the rows in the likelihood at creation, held, and re-derived only on request**
A count fit centres its forest's prior at the data's overall log rate, log(total count / total exposure), and dec-A140 took that total over every row, a row at weight 0 included, as a gaussian fit's response scale and a survival fit's span such rows; the alternative it weighed was the rows in the likelihood, recomputed whenever they change. Asked to mark it, the maintainer: "It seems as if individuals are entering and exiting, they're not always part of the population. What is actually correct here, from a modeling perspective?" Two things are. A quantity a prior is built on cannot move during a run: where membership is itself drawn by an outer sampler, a centre recomputed at each change makes the prior a function of the thing being sampled, which that sampler's membership step does not account for, so recomputing on every change is a sampler for no fixed model. And which fixed number it is, is a choice: every row depends only on what was observed and is off where the rows left out differ, by log 2 where half are structural zeros; the rows in the likelihood when the sampler is created is the number that makes a mask that never changes the same as deleting the rows, as in glm, and for a mask that changes it is fixed at the creation and can be refreshed in burn-in. That is the package's rule for derived data already (dec-B231, dec-B233): derived once, kept, re-derived when the caller says so. The orchestrator recommended it for every family and not the count fit alone, with the present rule, valid and documented, as the alternative if that was too wide before the merge. The maintainer on 2026-10-07: "Use your recommendation for this decision." The rule: what a fit takes from the response to place or size a prior, the count fit's centre, the gaussian and Student-t response scale, the survival fit's, and the unit a forest's sd is stated in, is computed over the rows in the likelihood when the sampler is created, a row at weight 0 or masked out not among them; it is held when weights or the mask change; and the setters that change them take the argument the response's setters take to re-derive it. Cut points are not in this: a row at weight 0 stays in the design and occupies a leaf. The cost is that every fit created with rows at weight 0 changes its prior and its draws, that a caller who starts with rows out and brings them in has a scale from the rows it started with, and a slice across families. Not built. Revises dec-A140. See also: [dec-A140], [dec-B231], [dec-B233], [dec-B277].
Record: root TODO, response-scale-rows. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B296]

**The sampler's own cut grids hold each value once**
The sampler builds a cut grid with equal points by itself: n.cuts identical points over a constant column, their range having no width, and neighbours that round to one number on a very narrow column or among quantile midpoints. Such grids are what a restore gets wrong (TODO repeated-cut-restore), and the plan for that fix kept them, as 0.9-34 has them, storing each split's position beside its value so that a restore is exact on any grid. Asked whether the sampler's own grids keep repeated points or hold one point for each value, with the first recommended, the maintainer: "I don't see the motivation for A. I doubt it matches a user's intent. Why do you recommend it?" The reasons had been that no fit changes, that it is less work, and that repeating a point to weight it could follow later; the first two are cost and the third holds either way, a caller's repeat being refused in 1.0-0 (dec-B285) and so new input later under both. On intent nobody means a hundred copies of one point: over a constant column they make the column look splittable, so splits are proposed on it that put every row on one side and are thrown away, and on a narrow column they make some thresholds twice as likely as others by rounding alone. The orchestrator withdrew the recommendation. The maintainer on 2026-10-07: "Yes, use your revised recommendation." The rule: a grid is a set of distinct thresholds, and every grid the sampler builds or accepts holds each value once. A stored split's value then names one position, so a restore needs no stored position while that holds; the part of the fix that keeps a split inside its node's interval after a grid changes stands. Three things follow and are not ruled: what a constant column's grid is and what it becomes when the column is later given values; what a refresh does when the values cannot supply the number of points asked for; and whether a state that carries a grid with repeats is refused or has them dropped. The cost is that a fit with a very narrow column, or a constant column beside missing values, draws differently, and that every place that builds or accepts a grid changes. Built (docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md). See also: [dec-B285], [dec-B231], [dec-A170].
Record: docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md, Landing note. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B297]

**A grid refreshed for a column takes up to n.cuts points from the new values, whatever it held before**
Under dec-B297 a constant column's grid is one point at its value. A caller who runs the sampler inside a larger one may create a column as a placeholder, dbarts(cbind(x, latent = 0), y), and give it values later; without updateCutPoints = TRUE the grid is kept, as derived data is (dec-B231). With it, the grid could take up to the n.cuts points the sampler was created with, from the new values, or the one point the column had at creation, which is what the quantile rule does today and what the prototype of dec-B297 did by counting a column's points from its grid. The second leaves the column nearly unsplittable for no reason its caller can see. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown the two: "Up to n.cuts, from the new values." The rule: a refresh of a column's grid derives it as a creation would for those values, up to the sampler's n.cuts, and the number of points a column held before does not enter. Built (docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md). See also: [dec-B297], [dec-B231], [dec-A170].
Record: docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md, Landing note. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B298]

**A grid refreshed onto values that cannot supply n.cuts distinct points gets the points they do supply**
A very narrow column may hold fewer distinct numbers than the n.cuts a sampler was created with, 5 where 100 were asked for. On a refresh, setPredictor with updateCutPoints = TRUE, the grid could shrink to the distinct points the values give, which is what a creation gives for those values under dec-B297 and dec-B298; the refresh could be refused, as the quantile rule's is today, which stops a loop that refreshes each sweep on a column that happens to be narrow; or the old grid could be kept in silence, the caller having asked for a refresh. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown the three: "Shrink: the grid gets the 5 distinct points." The rule: a refresh never fails for want of distinct points and never keeps the old grid; the grid is the distinct points the values supply, up to n.cuts, under the uniform rule and the quantile rule alike. Built (docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md). See also: [dec-B297], [dec-B298].
Record: docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md, Landing note. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B299]

**Cut-point weights will come as probabilities beside the grid, never as repeated points, and a state whose grid repeats a point is refused**
dec-B285 left open how a caller would later make some cut points likelier than others, by repeating a point or by an argument of probabilities, and refused a caller's repeat in 1.0-0 so that either could be added. With dec-B297, under which a grid is a set of distinct thresholds, the maintainer asked: "Are we going to allow users to manually repeat points later?" It was undecided. Repeats later would undo dec-B297, and would bring back the restore's defect, a stored split's value naming several positions, and with it the need to store each split's position; the package's idiom for weighting is already a vector of probabilities, split.probs for columns, and the same shape serves cut points, setCutPoints(cuts, column, probs = ), after the release. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, asked to settle both: "Weights later come as probabilities beside the grid, never as repeats" and "The state question answers itself: refuse". The rule: no grid repeats a point in any version, so a stored split's value always names one position and a restore by value stays exact with nothing more stored; weighting cut points, when it is built, is an argument of probabilities beside the grid; and a state that carries a grid with a repeated point, which only a hand-edited state or one saved by a development build can, is refused by setState, a copy and a reload, naming the repeat. The alternative for the state was dropping its repeats and installing, which moves the splits that sat on the dropped copies. Revises dec-B285 in part. Built (docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md). See also: [dec-B285], [dec-B297], [dec-B283].
Record: docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md, Landing note; root TODO, cut-point-weights. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B300]

**The count model's checks are reshaped, a calibration arm draws k, and the calibration suite is run by hand before the merge**
dec-A140 recorded three choices about checks made when the count model landed: the mixing check counts two chains constant at one shared value as agreeing; the calibration check holds the count fit's k fixed; and the reference fits were re-recorded for the count family alone, the rest carried after being verified identical. Asked to confirm them or tighten them, the maintainer: "I want a step-back on the testing choices: are they the right shape? Do they leave anything out? Are there other recommendations than the 'tighten'ing listed?" Read again at the scripts on 2026-10-07: one of the mixing check's two cases has a posterior on the shape that is a single value, so agreement cannot be measured there and the rule for constant chains is a patch on the case; both chains start from one value, where chains started apart are what shows a sampler that does not move; no correctness check of any family draws k, by calibration or by an exact gate, though a drawn k is the default for binary and count fits, the recorded baselines catching a change and not a wrong sampler; the k step depends on the leaf values alone, so one arm on the cheapest family tests it; and the calibration suite runs on a weekly schedule that does not fire until the workflow is on main, so on the branch it runs only when started by hand. The third choice is sound. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown keeping the three as built against reshaping: "Fix the shape." The rule: the mixing check takes a case whose posterior spreads over several values in place of the degenerate one, the rule for constant chains goes, and its chains start apart where the sampler lets the shape's start be set; the calibration harness gains one arm with k drawn under its default hyperprior, on probit; the carried reference fits stand; and running the calibration suite by hand and reading it is a step before the merge to main. The alternative of drawing k in the count arm costs run time for what the probit arm shows. Not built. Revises dec-A140 in part. See also: [dec-A140], [dec-A132], [dec-B296].
Record: root TODO, check-shape-fixes. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B301]

**A sampler created with no row in the likelihood takes its scale from every row, with a warning**
dec-B296 takes what the response gives a prior from the rows in the likelihood at creation, and a sampler created with every weight zero, or under a mask of zeros, has none. Run on 2026-10-07 at the tip: such a gaussian sampler is created and runs, with the warning that the starting sigma fell back to the marginal sd, which does not name the cause; lm returns missing coefficients without a word and glm fails. The caller seen doing this on purpose runs a mixture whose component starts with no members and gains rows later. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown creating it with a warning, creating it in silence and refusing: "Create it, scale from every row, warn." The rule: the sampler is created, each quantity of dec-B296 is computed over every row, and one warning says that no row is in the likelihood and every row was used. The alternatives were silence, which tells a caller who zeroed every weight by mistake nothing, and a refusal, which makes a mixture start every component with a row. The mixture's caller gets one warning per sampler for what was meant. A re-derivation asked for by updateScale = TRUE with no row in is a separate case and is refused, the design's call. Not built; in the design for response-scale-rows. See also: [dec-B296].
Record: root TODO, response-scale-rows. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07; revised in part by dec-B364. [dec-B302]

**The scale of a multiplier is taken from the rows in the likelihood at creation, held, and re-derived by updateBasisScale**
A forest that multiplies a numeric column takes its default sd per standard deviation of that column (dec-B261), and the plan for the multiplier slice counted every row the sampler holds in that standard deviation, rows of weight zero included, "as the response's scale does"; dec-B296 has since taken the response's scale from the rows in the likelihood. The orchestrator recommended every row, on the rule that what comes from the predictors spans every row, as cut points do (run on 2026-10-07: with five rows of weight zero far out, splits land past the range of the weighted rows) and as a linear leaf's covariate scale does, and on the ground that such a number never needs deriving again. The maintainer asked: "Is this another instance where we should allow one behavior for burn-in and one for after? Allowing it to shift and then be locked in? If so, we should match the rows at creation and have controlled rescaling on updates." It is: dec-B265 already holds the number from creation and re-derives it only by updateBasisScale, for burn-in, so nothing is saved by spanning every row, and a prior that sizes the column times the forest against the response's spread is coherent only when both are measured over the rows being fitted. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown the revised recommendation: "Yes, use your revised recommendation." The rule: the column's standard deviation is taken over the rows in the likelihood when the sampler is created, unweighted, and held; a later change of weights or of the mask does not move it; updateBasisScale = TRUE on setForestBasis re-derives it over the rows in at that call, so installing the column in force again with the argument is how a caller rescales after the weights change, and no argument is added to setWeights or setActiveRows for it; with no row in at creation it is taken over every row under dec-B302's warning. A column constant over the rows in is refused at creation where no sd is stated, though it varies over every row, by dec-B265's rule for a constant column. Until updateBasisScale is built, after the merge to main (dec-B276), the number stays that of creation's rows. A fit with rows of weight zero no longer takes this default from them, and differs from today's plan where their column values spread differently. Open, and next for the maintainer: a linear or gp leaf's covariate centre and scale, held and re-derived by updateStandardization, read every row today and are the same kind of number. Not built. Revises the plan's reading of dec-B261. See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B265], [dec-B296], [dec-B302], [dec-B276].
Record: docs/plans/forest-multiplier.md, Ruled after this plan was amended. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B303]

**A linear or gp leaf's covariate centre and scale are taken from the rows in the likelihood at creation, and held**
A linear or Gaussian-process leaf centres and scales its covariate before fitting, the slope's prior and the default lengthscale being stated on the scaled column, so the pair is the same kind of number as a multiplier's scale (dec-B303). Read in the source on 2026-10-07: the centre and scale are taken over every row the sampler holds, unweighted, rows of weight zero included; they are kept when a predictor's values change and derived again when the data are replaced; xbart's folds copy the full data's; and updateStandardization, which dec-B233 ruled as the argument that re-derives them, is not built. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown the rows in the likelihood at creation against every row, with the orchestrator recommending the first: "Agreed." The rule: the centre and scale, and the default lengthscale derived with them, are computed over the rows in the likelihood when the sampler is created, unweighted, and held; a later change of weights or of the mask does not move them; updateStandardization = TRUE re-derives them over the rows in at that call; with no row in at creation they are taken over every row under dec-B302's warning; and a fold of xbart takes them from its own training rows, as it takes the response's range. The alternative, every row as built, sizes the slope's prior by rows that are not fitted and under another rule than the multiplier's. Linear and gp fits created with rows of weight zero change their draws; leaf models are new in 1.0-0, so no released behaviour moves. Until updateStandardization is built the numbers stay those of creation's rows. Cut points still span every row. Not built; joins response-scale-rows. See also: [dec-B303], [dec-B296], [dec-B233], [dec-B231], [dec-B302].
Record: root TODO, response-scale-rows. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B304]

**setState installs a state only where it goes in cleanly, unless told to force**
dec-B234 gave setState a return value, and the build made it TRUE when nothing had to be changed to install the state and FALSE when anything was; the install itself always went through, merging a leaf no row reaches, dropping a missing-value side, converting leaf values stored in other response units (dec-A167). Asked whether such a conversion should give FALSE, and told he had ruled a return of exact or not, the maintainer answered: "Is that what I ruled? I thought I recommended that we have a way to 'cram' a state in there and patch up whatever we could, a force-update. If the force update was off, it would return a TRUE or a FALSE if the update could be done cleanly. Cleanly was defined as leaving a sampler in a coherent state with regards to its model." Read again: the words of 2026-10-05 were "Return a status, no warning", to a question the orchestrator had framed as exact or not; those of dec-B283 were that setState "can do what other set functions do: force an update to a reasonable coherent state or accept/reject if it is obviously broken", of which the build and the ledger took only that it does what it can, and the force argument went to installTrees alone (dec-B295). Run on 2026-10-07: a state restored after the response range moved returns FALSE with fitted values equal to the stored ones to 4e-15. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown the rule in setPredictor's idiom and asked whether unforced is the default: "Yes, unforced should be the default." The rule: setState(state, forceUpdate = FALSE) installs a state that fits the sampler's model and data as they stand and returns TRUE, and otherwise leaves the sampler untouched and returns FALSE; with forceUpdate = TRUE it installs, changing what it must. Clean is the sampler left coherent with its model: a conversion between response units is clean. Not clean is a tree that would have to be changed to fit, a leaf no row reaches or a split off the grid, and a store of kept draws larger than the sampler's (dec-B294, whose "installs what fits" is now the forced form). A state that is plainly broken, or of a shape that does not fit, is an error in both forms, as now (dec-B283). copy() and a reload force, having no choice. The alternative was the build: always install, and report whether anything changed. The cost is that code calling setState(st) and relying on its repairs now gets FALSE and an untouched sampler until it states forceUpdate = TRUE; that repair was never in a release, 0.9-34 installing a state as given. To design: what the forced form returns, so a caller can still tell a repaired install from an untouched one; whether a dropped missing-value side or re-derived latents count as clean; the name against installTrees's force argument (dec-B295), one name for both; and the callers in the package and in stan4bart, which calls setState. Not built. Revises dec-B234 and dec-A167; revises dec-B294 in part. See also: [dec-B234], [dec-B283], [dec-B294], [dec-B295], [dec-A167], [dec-B200].
Record: root TODO, setstate-force-update. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07; revised in part by dec-B318. [dec-B305]

**Where the cores cannot be counted the default thread count is one**
The default n.threads of the fitting functions is taken from guessNumCores(), which returns NA on a system whose cores it cannot count, and an NA thread count is refused; dec-A163 kept the refusal and made its message say why. Run on 2026-10-07: a stated n.threads = NA gives that message on the branch, and on 0.9-34 gives "'n.threads' must be a positive integer" with no cause. So on such a system a call that says nothing of threads fails. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown one thread in silence, one thread with a message and the refusal as built, the orchestrator recommending the first: "Fall back to one thread, silently." The rule: where n.threads is not stated and the cores cannot be counted, the fit runs on one thread with no message, at every function whose default is the guess (dbartsControl, bart, bartBT, rbart_vi, xbart); a stated n.threads that is NA stays refused, with dec-A163's message reworded to what a caller who wrote NA needs; guessNumCores() still returns NA; and the help for n.threads and for guessNumCores says the default is then one. The alternatives were a message on every such fit and the refusal. The cost is that a user on such a machine gets one thread and is not told more could be had by asking. Revises dec-A163. See also: [dec-A163], [dec-A182].
Record: docs/plans/small-r-batch.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B306]

**A constant basis column with a stated sd is created, until several forests with no basis are built**
A forest times a column that is constant is a second forest with no basis. Run on 2026-10-07 at the tip: forest(x2, basis = one, sd = 2), one being a column of ones, is created; forest(x2, basis = 1) is refused, a basis having a value for every observation; and forest(x1) + forest(x2) is refused, a model having one forest with no multiplier. The forest plans had left open whether the constant column with a stated sd is created, as dec-B265's letter has it, or refused as the other two spellings are, and the orchestrator recommended refusing, the likely case being a treatment column left constant by a subset. The maintainer asked: "Why are we refusing two plain forests? It's temporary, right?" It is: the refusal stands because two things are not designed, whose the fitting function's tree count and tree prior are among several such forests (dec-B274) and their prior, each taking the whole default making a sum of several times the variance where dec-B292 has them share one total, built after the merge and before the release. With sd stated the caller has set the forest's size by hand, which is what the missing design would decide; the column of ones is the one way to write forests that simply add until then, and was named as that when dec-B292 was ruled; and a caller who states sd wrote the forest on purpose. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown the revised recommendation: "Use your revised recommendation." The rule: a basis column that is constant is created where sd is stated, as built, and refused where it is not, there being no spread to take a default from (dec-B265, dec-B303). The alternative was the refusal. The cost is that a treatment column left constant by a subset, with sd stated, fits with no message. To revisit when several forests with no basis are built: whether the constant column stays a second spelling or sends the caller to the plain form. See also: [dec-B265], [dec-B292], [dec-B274], [dec-B303].
Record: root TODO, forests-shared-total. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B307]

**A factor basis drops a level no row has, without a message, however it is written**
A factor given as a basis may declare a level that no row the fit keeps has, by its own levels or because subset removed that level's rows, and since a basis factor has two levels in 1.0-0 (dec-B281) a factor of three with one of them empty is taken only if the empty level is dropped. Run on 2026-10-07 at the tip: written as a formula term, named in a forests list and handed to a forests list as a vector, such a factor is created with the two levels that have rows, no message; lm drops the level the same way and refuses a row with it at predict ("factor arm has new level c"). The plan for the slice that takes a basis by its class had a factor handed over as a value refused with "drop it with droplevels()", the same factor written as code still dropping the level, a call of its planning and not a ruling. The maintainer on 2026-10-07, shown dropping in every spelling against the planned refusal: "Use option A." The rule: at creation a factor basis is the levels that have a row among the rows kept, in every spelling and at every door that takes a factor, with no message; its size, two levels or more, is judged after that; and a later predict or basis swap that carries the dropped level is refused as a level the forest does not have (dec-B289). The alternative was the refusal for a value. The cost is that the caller is not told a level went. A level emptied after creation by a swap inside a larger sampler stays a level, as recorded. Not built as a rule; the tip does it. See also: [dec-B281], [dec-B289], [dec-B260].
Record: docs/plans/forest-kind-by-class.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B308]

**Fewer than two distinct response values in the likelihood at creation take the every-row fallback**
dec-B302 gives a sampler created with no row in the likelihood its scale from every row, with a warning. The design's critic measured the next case, one row in: the response's range is then a window of width one upward from that row's value, and once the other rows return the fit has root mean squared error 2.41 and a residual sd of 2.66, against 0.46 and 0.90 where no row is in or on the tip. The orchestrator widened the fallback to that case and told the maintainer, who answered on 2026-10-07: "That sounds fine.", and then: "I was just referring to your call about when there are fewer than two distinct response values in the likelihood at creation." The rule: where the rows in the likelihood at creation hold fewer than two distinct values of the response, each quantity of dec-B296 is taken over every row, under dec-B302's one warning, worded to be true of both cases. The alternative was the window of width one, as a constant response gives. Not built; in the plan for response-scale-rows. See also: [dec-B302], [dec-B296].
Record: root TODO, response-scale-rows. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B309]

**A forced update returns nothing: the return value says whether an unforced update was a valid one**
dec-B305 left open what setState returns when forced. The orchestrator recommended TRUE, as a forced setPredictor returns on the branch, on the ground that the value could then be tested in every call; a repair reported by FALSE was the other option shown. The maintainer asked what 0.9-34 did. Run on 2026-10-07 on that build: an unforced setPredictor returns TRUE when taken and FALSE when refused, the sampler unchanged; a forced one returns NULL invisibly, whether or not it collapsed anything (76 nodes to 44 in the run); its help calls the value "TRUE/FALSE depending on whether or not the operation was successful". The branch's TRUE from a forced setPredictor is a change from the release that no entry records. The maintainer, to the orchestrator's ground: "I don't think your motivation makes sense. if (!s$setState(st)) s$setState(st, forceUpdate = TRUE) works just fine if it returns NULL when forced, assuming forcing is not the default." And, ruling: "Make them NULL. The return value when not forced is an indicator of whether or not a statistically valid update was made. That is irrelevant when forcing, because that kind of validity has no value." The rule: the forced forms of setState, setPredictor and installTrees return NULL invisibly; the unforced forms return TRUE where the update was made and FALSE where it was not and the sampler is as it was. setPredictor's forceUpdate = "partial" keeps its vector by observation. The alternatives were TRUE, which only code passing the flag as a variable and testing the result would use, and FALSE for an install that needed a repair, which gives FALSE two meanings. A caller who forces is not told whether anything was changed; one who needs to know calls unforced first. Built for setPredictor (docs/plans/small-r-batch.md); the forced forms of setState and installTrees are not built. Settles dec-B305's open point. See also: [dec-B305], [dec-B295], [dec-B234].
Record: docs/plans/small-r-batch.md; root TODO, setstate-force-update and forced-update-returns-null. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B310]

**When a column's grid is replaced its splits keep their position, or move by value on request**
A tree's split is a place in its column's grid. When setPredictor re-derives the grid from new values, or setCutPoints installs a caller's own, something must be said of the splits already on the column, and since dec-B298 and dec-B299 the new grid can differ in length from the old. The plan kept each split's index and, on a shorter grid, dropped a split past its end when forced and returned FALSE with the old column and grid when not (dec-A178, its third call). Shown keeping the place, moving each split to the new point nearest its old threshold, and a mix by whether the length changed, with the second recommended, the maintainer: "They're both valid models, right? Either the split points actually defined relative to fixed values, or they're with regards to quantiles in general." They are; the first is a quantile only under the quantile rule, a position under the default rule being a share of the range, so that one row moved from 0.9 to 1.5 carried a step in the fit from 0.5 to 0.75 (run on 2026-10-07); and keeping the index is not the relative model once the length changes, where the place must be rescaled. The maintainer: "Why not let it be an option? Defaulting to value." and then "Oh, wait a sec, keep the default at relative so it stays the same as 0.9-34." For setCutPoints, shown the same choice against always by value and always by position: "The same choice as the refresh, same words." In 0.9-34 both calls keep the index, its refresh never changing a grid's length (read from its source). The rule: by default a split keeps its position in the grid, which with the length unchanged is the release's behaviour to the bit; on request it moves to the new point nearest its old threshold, by the mapping setData and a warm start use. That a position is rescaled to a grid of another length began as the orchestrator's reading of "relative" and is ruled for setCutPoints, below. At a refresh the release has no such case. At setCutPoints it has: 0.9-34 takes a grid of any length and keeps each split's index, a split past the end of a shorter grid being merged away, so there the default departs from the release whenever the length changes (a column of 100 points given 2 keeps 85 of 95 nodes rescaled and 43 with the index kept, in the review's run). The option the maintainer was shown for setCutPoints said the position is rescaled when the length changes and did not say the release keeps the index; shown the two again on 2026-10-07 with that run, rescaling as built against keeping the index as the release does, under which split 37 of 100 becomes split 37 of 200 and the word means one thing at a refresh and another here, the maintainer: "Rescale, as built." The cost of the default is that the fitted function follows the grid; of the option, that a column replaced by a rescaled copy of itself loses its splits. Revises dec-A178 in part. Built (docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md). See also: [dec-A178], [dec-B298], [dec-B299], [dec-B312].
Record: docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B311]

**updateCutPoints takes "none", "position" or "value", and setCutPoints takes splits**
dec-B311 needs a spelling. Shown an argument of its own beside updateCutPoints, recommended, and a value of updateCutPoints, for which the same method's forceUpdate = "partial" and base R's sapply(simplify = "array") are precedent, the maintainer: "given that if it were an argument, it would only make sense if `updateCutPoints` was `TRUE`, it should probably be the value of the existing argument. If `updateCutPoints` is a logical, a one-time warning should be issued, scheduled for removal in 1.1-0. I welcome suggestions on what its options should be." Offered "none", "position" and "value", "none" being base R's word for the choice that does nothing and "position" preferred to "relative", which reads either way: "Sure, \"none\", \"position\", and \"value\" works for me." The rule: setPredictor's updateCutPoints is "none", the default, the grid kept; "position", a new grid with each split keeping its position; or "value", a new grid with each split moved to the point nearest its old threshold. A logical is taken until 1.1-0 with a warning once per session, TRUE as "position" and FALSE as "none". setCutPoints takes splits = "position", the default, or "value", after updateState (dec-B311's "same words"). Three things are the orchestrator's and not ruled: an explicit FALSE warns as TRUE does; the words are matched as match.arg matches, an abbreviation taken; and setCutPoints stays forced. Built, the logical among the tombstones (docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md). See also: [dec-B311], [dec-B288], [dec-A03].
Record: docs/plans/repeated-cut-restore.md. Marked: ruled by the maintainer, 2026-10-07. [dec-B312]

**The default rule gives a column with few distinct values one cut point for each gap between them**
Under the default rule a column's grid is n.cuts points evenly spaced over its range, whatever its values: a 0/1 column holds 100 points from 0.01 to 0.99, and a column of ages 18 to 30 holds 100, 8 or 9 in each gap (run on 2026-10-07); under the quantile rule the same columns hold 1 and 12. The plan for dec-B297 read "the distinct points the values supply" as the evenly spaced points with equal neighbours dropped, which keeps those grids (dec-A178, its second call). Shown that reading against one point for each gap where a column has fewer distinct values than n.cuts, the second recommended, the maintainer: "What do other packages do?
